@@2177285 <h> Human Population Bomb is Ticking <p> The human population growth factor in global warming is being ignored says a Marlborough conservationist Tony Orman , also an author and immediate past chairman of the Council of Outdoor Recreation Associations of NZ ( CORANZ ) . <p> His comment was made following the just concluded United Nations global-warming talks in Paris . <p> " The world 's most senior politicians have debated ways to combat accelerated climate change . But it 's voluntary and besides it 's a sticking plaster approach - treat symptoms but ignore the cause . There 's one very important undebated factor - people " he said . <p> There seemed an inherent fear about addressing the cause of accelerated global warming . Politicians and bureaucrats would use any scapegoat . A bizarre example was in 2007 when director general of the Department of Conservation Al Morrison , tried to incriminate wild deer alleging they were guilty of farting and belching . <p> " Animals ca n't argue back in defence , people do . Deer and cows do n't vote but people do . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ politics , " said Tony Orman . <p> " Humans or more particularly numbers of people , are the primary cause of environmental degradation and global warming . The more people , the more demand for resources . More people require more meat and milk - more cows . More people means more cars which means more emissions . Gimmee more , more and more " . <p> New Zealand has attempted to weakly and naively contribute by a free-market dollar-driven carbon trading called the Emissions Trading Scheme ( ETS ) . <p> Tony Orman said motorcar emissions needed to be addressed by lowering motorcar use . New Zealand has one of the highest car ownership rankings , 9th compared to the UK 33rd . <p> " Only long term investment in efficient public transport systems thereby reversing the escalation of car commuter mileages in cities like Auckland , Christchurch and Wellington , will bring motorcar usage and consequently emissions down " he said . <p> Instead governments and Auckland mayors ignorantly proceeded with building more motorways simply to accommodate more cars . Ignored was the root cause of environmental ills @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ had been well identified in past decades . Controversial author Paul Erlich warned about the people crisis in his book " The Population Bomb " published in 1968 and in New Zealand in 1970 , a few enlightened environmentalists such as " Save Manapouri " conservationist and deerstalkers association president John B Henderson publicly questioned " surely we can not argue for there being any virtue in further proliferating the hordes of humanity ? " <p> Sadly the policy of " Growth for Growth 's Sake " dominates . <p> Even the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change ( IPCC ) briefly commented acknowledging that " Globally , economic and population growth continued to be the most important drivers of increases in CO2 emissions from fossil fuels . " But that was lost in the clouds of hot air emitted by the global warming debate . And it will be lost in the " smoke-screens and mirrors " at Paris . <p> In New Zealand , successive prime ministers , cabinet ministers and metropolitan mayors seemed oblivious the country 's population is approaching five million and still blithely boasted of GDP and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ population policy . More , more and more - an addictive disease , " said Tony Orman . <p> The planet can not tolerate infinite growth . @@2179660 <h> Editorial : A year of scepticism , but also hope <p> 2015 was the year of Donald Trump in the United States , but there were signs of hope around the world too . <p> OPINION : The year -- another one -- ebbs away , and what will be left of 2015 once it 's gone ? <p> In Wellington , this was a year of endings : the year the super-city proposal went down in flames , and the storied department store Kirks announced it was closing down , and the Basin Reserve flyover was binned for good . <p> A strong vein of grass-roots scepticism had greeted some of these , as well as all kinds of other ideas : a new panda , a cycle lane , an airport runway . <p> Are we really so negative , though ? Can we not get anything done ? Not quite -- the sod was finally turned on Transmission Gully in 2015 , a road that has been preceded by eons of talking . Wellington City Council announced a winning convention centre and film museum @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , when an earlier plan fell spectacularly apart . <p> On the national stage , there was stasis -- or even regression -- sometimes too . An award-winning young adults book was temporarily banned for what amounted to indecency -- Lady Chatterley 's Lover , 2015 edition . The police upheld their tradition of unlawful searches . And the prospect of a new flag that erased the Union Jack was greeted with widespread , if slightly perplexing , anger . <p> New Zealand prison inmates assumed a new prominence this year , whether in fights filmed at a privately-run Auckland prison , or on cellphone calls from Christmas Island , where Australia has shipped its unwanted inhabitants . ( Australia 's unwanted , meanwhile , this year included Tony Abbott , the latest in a queue of that country 's prime ministers ritually sacrificed by their own party ) . <p> Elsewhere in New Zealand politics , Winston Peters stalled the National Party election juggernaut in Northland , while Prime Minister John Key pulled a ponytail , pitched a tent in the All Blacks ' dressing room , and goofed around @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . In other words , he remained a political phenomenon . <p> Just as phenomenal , this year , were the flagship sports teams : the All Blacks a model of ruthless professionalism at this year 's Rugby World Cup , and the Black Caps a study in boldness and heart en route to their first world cup final . <p> Did life change much in New Zealand in 2015 ? Not much , perhaps , but there were stirrings of what might happen yet . Before her death , Wellington lawyer Lecretia Seales made a powerful plea for a right to die . The union leader Helen Kelly , who also has terminal cancer , made her own articulate case for loosening the rules around marijuana access for the sick and dying . <p> Zooming out again , the world in 2015 seemed to lurch from one horror to another : endless civil war in Syria ; a wave of millions of refugees ; the persistence of the butcher-zealots of Isis ; terror attacks from Paris to Tunisia ; and the rise of Donald Trump . <p> Still , there @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ever , each vivid disaster masked deeper and more encouraging trends , like an ongoing reduction in extreme poverty , eradicable diseases and childhood mortality around the world . Even on the challenge that probably poses the deepest risk to the world , there was a start . The Paris climate talks might yet end up being the story of 2015 -- the moment when the world finally resolved to confront climate change . If so , it will have been a good year indeed .

@@2182800 <h> Breaking News <h> Giant sea spiders call Antarctica home <p> In order to test their theory , the team had to catch some of the spindly-legged creatures ( PycnoWatchThis/YoutTube ) <p> As tempting as it may be to escape the New Zealand heat and pop down to Antarctica for a holiday , new research reveals arachnophobes should probably stay away . <p> It turns out its frigid polar waters might be the ideal home to help sea spiders grow abnormally large -- some of the ones found in Antarctica have a leg-span of around 25cm . <p> Antarctic waters have helped more than just the usually small sea spiders live large , with copepods , echinoderms and certain mollusks also growing larger than their cousins living in warmer waters , according to Hakai Magazine . <p> The team of researchers from the United States Antarctic Program , National Science Foundation , University of Montana and University of Hawaii at Manoa hypothesised so-called " polar gigantism " was caused by the higher concentration of oxygen in cold water compared with warm water . Antarctica 's water , they @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Increased oxygen concentration in chilly water is believed to combine with cold slowing their metabolism to help the sea spiders and other creatures grow supersized -- a slower metabolism means they require less oxygen to survive , so it 's speculated they 're putting all that extra oxygen to good use by growing freakishly large . <p> In order to test their theory , the team had to catch some of the spindly-legged creatures . A hole was drilled in the thick sea ice near McMurdo Station before two specially-suited scuba divers went for a dip in water hovering between -1.5degC and -1.8degC -- the freezing temperature of seawater . <p> The research , published in Hakai , tested the sea spiders in a variety of temperatures and water with varying levels of oxygen dissolved in it . <p> So far they 've found larger sea spiders do n't fare well in low oxygen water , seeming to support their hypothesis . <p> It 's believed the system in Antarctica works especially well because instead of having gills or lungs , sea spiders absorb oxygen through diffusion . One researcher @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ organisms unless there 's a lot of oxygen available . <p> The team 's research suggests climate change could put pressure on many marine invertebrates , as temperatures rise and oxygen levels fall . <p> With studies showing Antarctica may be irreversibly damaged by climate change , it 's good to know at least it means there wo n't be more giant sea spiders roaming the world .

If there 's one cast iron rule of politics in New Zealand it is that most farmers vote National . Quite why is a mystery . It is Labour governments that have repeatedly <p> saved them . <p> Whether it was keeping farmers on their land in the 1930s depression , assisting returned soldiers on to the land after World War Two , weaning farmers off subsidies in the 1980s or setting up Fonterra in the <p> 2000s , Labour governments have done far more for farmers ' long term interests than any National government . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Farmers membership that showed more than 80 per cent think the National government is doing a good job is n't surprising when you look at it against the cast <p> iron law , but it does n't add up if you also think that farmers are reasonably intelligent people . <p> Admittedly , the survey was sent out to the Feds ' 12 000 members and only 1000 responded . But then most political surveys are based on a similar number , so it should be representative , if only of Fed <p> Farmers members . It needs to be remembered that only roughly a third of all farmers are represented by the Feds , although I am not suggesting there is a rabidly left wing coterie of farmers out there waiting <p> to rise up ( but I can live in hope ) . <p> The reasons given for why farmers are so happy with the government are low interest rates , the signing of the TPPA and the Paris agreement on climate change . <p> Low interests rates ? This might shave a bit off the cost of debt @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ massive loss of income dairy farmers have suffered this year . <p> The lower , and falling , dollar might be a better cause for hope , but that only works when markets are opened up . Which brings me to the TPPA . If you 're a farmer , you 've got nothing to crow about there - <p> the deal on dairy does nothing for the New Zealand dairy sector , and sheep and beef do n't do a lot better . <p> And climate change ? New Zealand 's commitments were some of the weakest of any developed country in the world . If you 're a farmer who thinks we do n't need to do anything to reduce our carbon emissions , then sure you might be happy . But if you 're serious about leaving a viable industry to future generations , you will be deeply concerned . <p> The arrival of a new year reminds us the future is never far away . It 's also a reminder we can never stop preparing for a better future .

" Our first Christmas celebration was a group of people who did not know each other very well . The other night we had 110 people -- it was like a big family reunion and they were all very proud . " <p> McLean believed the philanthropic model was a way forward for national conservation . <p> Project Janszoon is funded by the Next Foundation . Auckland couple Annette and Neal Plowman contributed $100m in 2014 to establish the charitable foundation to kick start high impact environmental and educational projects . <p> McLean said @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ case which encouraged the Plowman 's to establish the foundation . McLean previously worked with the family trust to restore the 83-hectare Rotoroa Island in Auckland 's Hauraki Gulf . <p> In early December the foundation announced the 34,000 ha Project Taranaki Mounga which aims to make Egmont National Park the first predator-free national park . <p> The question of who would fund the projects long term was answered when the Government and the Foundation signed the Tomorrow Accord last year . <p> The agreement commits the Government to maintaining qualifying projects once they have achieved agreed ecological indicators . <p> " We are having discussion with the Department of Conservation now focussing on the indicators , " McLean said . <p> Such markers would include a project 's proven ability to sustain sensitive species , such as mohua or northern rata -- both of which are highly susceptible to pests and predators . Success with weed species would demand their eradication . <p> Both parties were keen to see the model work , McLean said . <p> " We use philanthropic funds to the best advantage and take more risks @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ risks and we can apply that to the Taranaki project . <p> " We need to attract other philanthropists to see the benefits . I think philanthropy will always be a relatively small source of funding for what is needed - the guys with the deep pockets are central and local government . <p> " But if we can show what is practical I think it is the way forward -- while all the time having an eye on the predator-free dream through a landscape-style method . " <p> Landscape-style conservation was illustrated in Project Taranaki Mounga , which climbs from sea level to 2500m . It is described as the country 's first climate change resilient conservation project , because species could shift altitude to suit temperature changes . <p> " We need more sites like this in New Zealand so we can secure the majority of terrestrial biodiversity . Twenty years down the track the Government will be spending money shifting coastal infrastructure not on conservation . " <p> McLean said 2016 signalled a continuation of the project 's war on weeds , a northern rata planting programme , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , work on replanting the mobile coastal dunes at Anchorage , continued work on understanding pest trapping and pest habitat and the concept of a snail sanctuary at Canaan . <p> " I think we can achieve a surprising amount in a short time -- and I would really like to thank everyone who has put their shoulder to the wheel . The way the wider community is responding is really exciting . "

@@4883277 <h> Gordon Campbell on the view from Shibuya Crossing , Tokyo <p> December 3rd , 2015 <p> Sorry for being out of radio contact overseas for much of this week , even as the Cunliffe/Mahuta duo were being deep-sixed by that feisty Labour leader Andrew Little . ( Actually , I 'll believe " feisty " when Little says " goodnight and goodbye " to Annette King , as good as she may be in her role as Queen Mother of the Labour caucus . ) A small step of utu for Cunliffe is not exactly a giant step for mankind , or for Labour 's prospects in 2017 , when it will need all the talent it can muster . <p> For the next two fortnight or so , this will be a combined travel &amp; politics column , since I happen to be in Tokyo right now , as a first time traveller to Japan . Some things do n't change , though . After dining last night at a restaurant called Gonpachi in the Rippongi area of the city , I glanced up at the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with a raft of his celebrity guest diners down the years -- Sylvester Stallone , Steven Tyler etc- Right under the Stevie Wonder photo was the familiar smiling face of .... ' John Key , Prime Minister of New Zealand . ' There is no escape . The Gonpachi dining area BTW , served as the film set for Uma Thurman 's epic final battle scenes in the Tarantino film Kill Bill -- and hey , if anyone 's listening in , that 's not a coded reference to our Finance Minister . <p> Enough already about the wondrous public transport rail system , the neon brilliance of night-time , the lamp-posts that randomly blare out the Star Wars main theme ... and the patience and courtesy that 's coded right into the language . Entering and leaving a dining place can leave you feeling as if you 've just won the Thank You lottery , and -- amazingly -- a " thank you " in Japanese signifies both hierarchies and tenses . ' Arigatou ' is good to go for an informal ' thanks , " arigatou gozaimasu ' seems @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- if I 've got the spelling right- ' arigatou gozaimashita ' is ' thanks for what you did beforehand , like that thing you did yesterday . ' And supposedly , there 's a version of ' thanks ' specially reserved for the person holding the buttons so that you can get into/out of an elevator . So anyway ... " arigatou ' for bearing with my wonder at a language where the forms of gratitude convey a notion of time , and a sense of occasion . <p> But down to business . The media here in Tokyo faces the usual pressures , both economic and political . Yet there are two excellent English language compilations of original news and stuff gleaned from the likes of Yomiuri Shimbun -- of sufficient depth to show that really good journalism is still happening every day here . Among the highlights that may , or may not have made the news back home , and in no particular order : <p> 1 . The China Syndrome . Like New York and Paris , Tokyo is struggling to come to terms with the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the city into tourism theme parks . The process involves the repackaging of history and culture once held dear -- and certainly not because they can now be monetised . For obvious reasons , the influx of Chinese tourists and capital has been especially difficult for some Tokyo residents . <p> As a mirror reflection of this disquiet , the term ' bakugai ' has just topped the list of buzzwords for the year at this week 's U-Can award ceremonies for Top New Words and Buzzwords , run by a local publishing house . The term ' bakugai ' means : " explosive shopping spree " carried out by Chinese tourists , and it refers to practices such as the hiring of local mega-department stores for one hour by Chinese tour groups for their own exclusive use . ( And its not only Japanese nationalists who resent the growing hegemony of Chinese spending power . ) A runner up phrase in the same competition was " Abe seiji wo yurusanai " which means " We will not tolerate Abe 's policies " -- and refers in particular , to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> 2 . Women &amp; Paid Work : Policy Not Working . Enhancing the labour market participation by women is one of Abe 's ' five arrows ' of reform for the stagnant Japanese economy . Unfortunately ... on Wednesday , a Jiji Press headline : " Women Hold A Record 3.5% of Government Managerial Posts " shows just how far that campaign has to go , in order to get anywhere near its supposed target of 30% by 2020 . <p> A day later , an 82 page Abe government report on gender employment barriers in Japan pinpointed the usual substantive reasons : such as an ' out-dated family model in which men work long hours while women take care of the home . " This ' out-dated ' corporate culture has reportedly ' made it difficult for women to fully execute their abilities , or for men to participate in home-making , domestic duties and child-rearing . " Undaunted , the Abe government is about to release its fourth basic plan on the issue . The suggested solutions include placing caps on overtime hours , and encouraging people to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ which is nominally available but rarely uptaken in today 's competitive work environment . Abe , of course , can not take the lead and actually advocate for women to be spared or helped in their domestic/childrearing duties -- the old guys in Abe 's Liberal Democratic Party ( LDP ) would think he 'd lost his cojones if he tried . <p> 3 . TPP Again ! The TPP is not old news here . On Tuesday , a Japan Times editorial analysed the package of measures proposed by the Abe government to help ease the transition for businesses -- small and large -- once the TPP takes effect . In New Zealand of course , we 've been more inclined to allow the law of the free market jungle to weed out the frail from the fit -- and the more brutal the transition , the more bracingly healthy we 've tend to regard the winnowing out to be . In that respect , free market /free trade policies have been pretty much our own version of the Hunger Games ... <p> That 's not quite how they do @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ matter , which -- from the Kennedy era onwards -- has regularly required by law an evaluation ( and in many cases , a level of compensation ) of the impact that new trade pacts will have on jobs and business . <p> Abe is proposing just such a package -- though as yet , as the Japan Times says , its hard as yet to distinguish the rhetoric of assistance , from the actual , workable provisions of care . While government assistance is supposedly on the way for small and medium scale business harmed by the TPP , the Times notes that much of the package is aimed at handsomely compensating farmers and fisheries industries : <p> The outline contains fairly concrete steps to support domestic producers likely to face stiff competition amid the increased imports , and possibly suffer income losses . The steps include government purchases of the same amount of rice from domestic farmers as Japan will be obliged to import under the TPP deal , and raising the rate for covering the losses incurred by beef and pork producers from the current 80 percent to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , this can be too much of a good thing . In the wake of the WTO Uruguay Round of the 1990s , the government compensation package was apparently so generous , re-adjustment to market realities never really happened . Clearly a Goldilocks balance has to be struck -- somewhere between the cold turkey approach favoured by New Zealand , and the warm comfy cushion model favoured by Japan . <p> 4 . King Coal . Finally , a scary subtext to the Paris climate change talks . Amusingly , Obama called for binding rules -- not binding targets , but binding reviews of those non-binding targets . That would be funny , if it was n't so pathetic . <p> The shine is coming off once bright prospects for natural gas as the future fossil fuel of choice in Asia as power companies in India and Southeast Asia tap abundant and cheap domestic coal resources to generate electricity . <p> Asian loyalty to coal is shrinking the space available for natural gas just as supplies are ramping up after massive investments in U.S. and Australian output . Demand growth for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , further dampening the fuel 's prospects . <p> While much attention has been given to a potential peak in China 's coal demand and worries about emissions , in Asia alone this year power companies are building more than 500 coal-fired plants , with at least a thousand more on planning boards . Coal is not only cheaper than natural gas , it is often available locally and has no heavy import costs . <p> Meaning : there may be some hope for Solid Energy , but not so much for the planet . <p> Culture shocku : <p> I 've linked to Harajuku-based J-pop star Kyary Pamyu Pamyu before , but since she 's playing gigs in Tokyo next week , its a good time to see this astounding video for " PonPonPon " again : <p> And since Exile , the 19-member Japanese boy band , is also playing a big arena gig here in Tokyo this week , here 's one of their videos . As though dudes think they need a positive affirmation before the no . @@4889957 <p> Days Bay resident Sarah Gilbert braves the waves at Katherine Mansfield 's old holiday house at Days Bay , which was damaged in the record-breaking 2013 storm . Such extremes are expected once a year if sea level rises by 30cm . <p> When monster waves thrashed Wellington 's South Coast on June 21 , 2013 , they delivered an opportunity along with the mountains of debris . <p> The 100-year storm gouged 50m of Island Bay 's sea wall , handing a gift to planners wanting to bolster the South Coast 's resilience to predicted sea level rises of 30cm within 50 years . <p> The long-term solution would have replaced the wall and coastal road with sand dunes as a more effective buffer against the swells . But after two years of dogged debate , the wall is going right back where it was , with a bit of added wave-fending grunt . The bigger debate has been put off for another day . <p> The failed fight illustrates how hard it will be for councils around the country to act on Parliamentary Commissioner for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Preparing New Zealand for Rising Seas . <p> Wright predicts that , in Wellington , high tide peaks of the magnitude seen in that 2013 storm will happen once a year with a 30cm sea level rise . With a jump of 70cm , they will happen at every tide . <p> Across Kilbirnie , Wellington 's central business district , the Hutt River floodplain and Porirua Harbour , 104 homes and businesses are low-lying enough to be swamped by a sea level rise of 50cm . A total of 5008 homes , 160 businesses and 58km of road lie less than 1.5m above spring high tide . So councils need more detailed modelling to turn the PCE predictions into useable hazard maps . <p> Wellington Regional Council hazards analyst Iain Dawe has a doctorate in coastal processes and is charged with drilling down the regional impact of swelling seas . <p> Trouble is , the sea does n't graze evenly along the coast . It nibbles at some beaches , takes great bites out of coastal cliffs and then regurgitates its lunch onto other sandy shores . <p> Accurate hazard mapping for individual bays and even individual houses means knowing whether a beach is eroding or filling out , how sediment moves along the coast , how climate change might alter exposure to road-topping waves . That work is underway - Wellington is lucky to have enough historical data to enable that , Dawe says . But in shaky Wellington even predicting base sea level change is tricky . <p> The capital has one of New Zealand 's highest rates of sea level rise @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 15 years , it 's been more like 4mm a year , because plate boundary subsidence is dragging us down . If that continues , " that changes the picture hugely " . <p> Wellington Harbour is particularly vulnerable , because it has such a small tidal range , Dawe says . Infrastructure built in the 1930s , 40s and 50s , to withstand 85cm of tidal flux , is already being stressed with the 20cm of sea level rise accumulated over the past century . Throw another 30cm on top of that and roads , sea walls , train tracks could be more regularly breached . <p> The PCE map shows low-lying areas of the Wellington region . <p> Across the region , the most risk-prone areas include Palliser Bay , Wellington 's South Coast and road and rail infrastructure , the Hutt River floodplain , the Eastbourne road , Pauatahanui Inlet and Porirua Harbour . <p> Former Environment Court judge Joan Allin calls for care in any risk assessment . Instead , they should give best estimates of likely outcomes , or show the range of possibilities . Councils should release expert reports in draft form for community consultation and recognise there is a cost to being overly cautious , as well as to ignoring real risks , she says . <p> " You 've got to get the right balance , and in order to do that you actually need proper , objective , scientific information , not very unlikely results . In dry weather , they are skate parks , sports fields and tiered seating . But when it rains they act as holding lakes , preventing surface flooding and allowing water to drain out to sea . <p> Stilt homes <p> Studio 2 Auckland architect Paul Clarke says clients still want to build on coastal properties , on the basis they 're prepared to take the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ get in the meantime . <p> But others are factoring the risk into their designs . On a Great Barrier Island floodplain , he completed a stilt house that sits about 1m above the land . The timber treated piles , which cost about the same as the standard piles for a normal bungalow , could also be used on a coastal site . <p> He 's finishing a project at Hahei in the Coromandel using hollow timber driven piles filled with poured concrete for extra stability . <p> His previous firm , Crosson Clarke Carnachan , designed a house on sleds ( pictured ) that can be moved around a site to accommodate coastal change . <p> Moveable homes <p> One sea level rise management suggestion is for councils to allow only removable homes on land threatened by sea level rise . <p> Prefab NZ chief executive Pamela Bell says New Zealand has a long history of carting homes around the country . <p> Any house on piles can be moved , as shown by her own Te Horo hut ( pictured ) , which they transported from Auckland .

@@4892103 <h> What drives urban change in NZ ? - report <p> A new report about the drivers of urban change in New Zealand by the MBIE-funded Resilient Urban Futures Programme has been launched in Parliament by MPs Nicky Wagner and Grant Robertson . The report ' Drivers of Urban Change ' brings together the views of key decision-makers and cutting-edge research . <p> It looks at issues such as compact versus dispersed urban development and infrastructure renewal , resilient transport patterns and healthy , affordable housing . <p> The editors , Lisa Early , Philippa Howden-Chapman and Marie Russell , drew on interviews with over 90 stakeholders in the policy-making process carried out by Marie Russell and Guy Salmon . <p> " We looked at how our cities and towns can provide a desirable , prosperous , socially inclusive , healthy and environmentally sustainable way of life , " says Professor Philippa Howden-Chapman , Director of the NZ Centre for Sustainable Cities hosted by the University of Otago , Wellington . <p> The report includes the results of a nationwide opinion poll about social attitude to urban issues @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of social , demographic and economic trends , and environmental challenges . <p> " Our cities face common themes and issues , such as environmental challenges , ranging from pollution to water quality and quantity , as well as hazards such as floods and earthquakes , climate change , and how cities aim to achieve resilience and sustainability , " Howden-Chapman says . <p> The research was funded by the Ministry of Business , Innovation and Employment and focused on five urban centres - Auckland , Hamilton , the Wellington Region ( Wellington , Porirua , Upper Hutt , Hutt City , Kapiti Coast ) , Christchurch and Dunedin . Each of these centres has diverse and specific challenges , and possibly quite different future development trajectories . <p> " Several cities in our study have a strong local political mandate for a quality , compact city with walkable neighbourhoods and good public transport . Yet there have been difficulties in translating this vision to reality , " she says . <p> The report looks at progress made and barriers encountered in attempting to implement these changes in our cities . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , go to http : **75;0;TOOLONG

Worldwide installed capacity is forecast to double this decade . " <p> The GGA 's objectives are to reduce risks associated with geothermal business , encourage geothermal investment and help integrate geothermal facilities into energy markets . It also promotes ways of using geothermal resources and associated network infrastructure efficiently .

There is growing recognition that policy makers need to address the important but conflicting challenges of mitigating climate change as well as expanding food production , " says Dr Rolleston . <p> The Food and Agriculture Organisation of the United Nations estimates that food production will need to expand by 60% by 2050 , and Dr Rolleston says that a meaningful agreement ca n't happen without obligations to control greenhouse gas emissions also falling on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ their people . <p> " While issues such as financing and technology transfer from rich countries to developing countries are central to the negotiations , farmers from throughout the world have been discussing the issue of better productivity and therefore carbon efficiency to play their part in meeting these challenges . " <p> " The effects of a changing climate also threatens food security through pests and diseases , as well as changing weather patterns in particular drought and floods , " Dr Rolleston says . <p> Developing countries , who have similar emissions profiles to New Zealand in that a significant portion of their emissions are from agriculture are seeking assurance that food production will not be compromised . They seek help to build resilience as well as the assurance that the agreement itself will facilitate rather than inhibit their ability to produce food . <p> Dr Rolleston says , " This is the message Federated Farmers has been saying for some time . It is a message common to farmers across the world who are united in their view . The Tritium fast chargers draw up to 400 volts of electricity and can charge a standard electric vehicle in the time it takes a customer to buy and drink a cup of coffee . <p> Z Sustainability Manager , Gerri Ward , said the move was part of Z 's continued commitment to moving from being a part of the climate change problem to the heart of the solution . <p> " We 're not an oil company , we 're a transport energy company and we 're committed to meeting the needs of our customers , whatever they might be . <p> " We also want to be at the front of the push towards a cleaner , more sustainable New Zealand and to give Kiwis choices to use more renewable fuels . " <p> The electric charging stations @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of users ( i.e. a Nissan Leaf ) to fill up , and the charge time from empty will be approximately 10 -- 25 minutes , as opposed to up to eight hours for a conventional slow charge . A 25 minute charge will " fill up " an entry level electric vehicle like a Leaf and allow customers to travel around 120 kilometers . <p> " These stations provide another option to keep our customers moving regardless of the vehicle they drive or the fuel they need , " said Gerri . <p> At the time that these stations are available for the public , Z will also be commissioning a $26 million investment in the country 's first commercial-scale biodiesel plant in Wiri , Auckland . The plant will convert inedible tallow into 20 million litres of high-quality sustainable biodiesel per year . <p> Chief Executive Steve West from Charge.net.nz has found working with Z Energy to be a great experience , and is pleased to see the Kiwi brand charging into the future . <p> " Z Energy is a great company to work and collaborate with . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " he said .

The COP21 meeting , made up of 196 countries - including New Zealand - which are part of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change , have just struck a deal to limit global warming to a 2 degrees Celsius increase by 2030 . <p> Why should I care ? <p> CHARLES PLATIAU/REUTERS <p> The slogan " No Plan B " is projected on the Eiffel Tower as part of the Paris climate talks . <p> In the words of our Climate Change Minister Tim Groser , it 's " the first truly global agreement on climate change " as it covers all countries , not just the wealthy ones . <p> Recent meetings to hammer out a climate change target , such as at Kyoto and Copenhagen , have @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reductions . <p> What exactly have they agreed to ? <p> On top of the 2C target , they have promised to raise $100 billion a year by 2020 to help poor countries adapt their economies , and accepted a new goal of net zero emissions by " later this century " . <p> Why is 2C warming such a big deal ? What will happen if temperatures go up that much ? <p> Nothing too pleasant . The current global temperature is a little over 1C above the pre-industrial level . <p> A study by the University of Anglia and the Met Office in the United Kingdom said the volume of carbon dioxide accumulated in the atmosphere ( a different measurement than emission rates of CO2 ) was about two-thirds of the level needed to avoid exceeding the two-degree barrier . <p> That 's not quite true : while all countries have signed on to the pledge , the current emissions reduction goals from most of them wo n't be enough to keep global warming to less than 2C . The goals are also not legally binding , although countries @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ keep to them . <p> Not that well : Green Party co-leader James Shaw says the Government 's current target of a 30 per cent reduction below the CO2 level in 2005 by the year 2030 ( an emission rate 11 per cent below our 1990 output ) is " clearly out of line " with the Paris agreement . <p> So we 're doomed then . <p> Not yet : while the current targets are weak , Shaw says the deal includes a requirement to measure progress and update pledges every five years , which means countries should get more aggressive in their emission reduction ambitions . <p> What do NZ businesses make of the deal ? <p> They seem pretty happy , actually . The Sustainable Business Council says there 's a lot of business goodwill for progress on the climate , while other companies or industry groups like Mainfreight say they 're already putting a lot of effort into reducing emissions . <p> Which sector will feel the most pressure to help the climate ? <p> Probably agriculture , which was responsible for 48 per cent of New @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ our emissions trading scheme . <p> Federated Farmers vice-president Anders Crofoot says the industry is already making " huge investment " into research on how to reduce emissions , but that progress takes time . <p> He also points to the economic importance of agriculture to New Zealand , and says we 're actually " quite efficient " producers in terms of greenhouse gas emissions compared to agricultural sectors in other countries . <p> Anyone or anything else under the pump ? <p> Our use of fossil fuels for energy and transport , which made up 39 per cent of our 2013 emissions , is also in the spotlight . Kiwi climate scientists like Professor Martin Manning say the deal gives a " clear signal " that fossil fuels need to be phased out in the next few decades in favour of renewable energy ( think electric cars instead of gas-guzzlers ) . <p> So how will Kiwi businesses be hit by this ? And what about me ? <p> It 's a little too early to say : this is a high-level agreement which has set the 2C goal , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ individual climate change policies to move towards that target . <p> The Government is already reviewing its emissions trading scheme , which has been in place since 2008 to meet its climate change targets . However , it 's already ruled out bringing agricultural emissions into the ETS . <p> Climate Change Minister Tim Groser says most Kiwis will notice the impact of the Paris deal at the petrol pump and on their electricity bills , when a likely " tightening up " of the ETS leads to a rise in carbon prices and a flow-on effect for energy costs . <p> What happens next ? <p> Each country has to ratify the agreement and provide its emissions reduction target ( or " nationally determined contribution " . @@4907562 <h> Largest patch of blue on the globe <p> It 's sometimes easy to forget in the suburbs of Dunedin that we are all adhered to the shaky side of a remote , isolated island chain , a spiky planet somewhat inaccurately named Earth . <p> Simon Winchester has not attempted an encyclopaedic approach to his work , for obvious reasons . <p> The Pacific Ocean contains multitudes . <p> He has wisely ( and entirely successfully ) collected , or perhaps curated , a series of snapshots of the intersection of humanity with the ecology and geography we make our lives within . <p> What made it most rewarding was the author 's dry humour , and sense of the absurd , leavened by a great sympathy for the human-scale trials and tribulations of those who have lived on and around that other vast ocean . <p> Could @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ he has . <p> Equally compelling and a worthy companion volume , Pacific is structured around a series of loosely connected chapters , dealing with events starting from January 1 , 1950 ( the reason for this can be found in the book ) . <p> Each chapter deals with an aspect of his topic , including nuclear testing and its dire effects , the genesis of Japanese consumer electronics , a history of surfing , the tensions on the Korean Peninsula , the decolonisation of Southeast Asia , the Pacific weather systems and climate change , Australia 's politics and society , volcanic vents in the abyssal deep , the Great Barrier Reef , and the militarisation of the Pacific by the United States and its new rival China . <p> In each of these sections , the author develops his themes in a discursive way , delving back into history , mulling on the future , diverting down curious byways . <p> He is just as adept at providing a potted explanation of El Nino as recounting the cultural politics behind the development of the Sydney Opera House . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and the author brings the eye of a novelist to his narrative . <p> It would be a strange person who would not be moved and inspired by the grand picture he has created . <p> Above all , what comes through is the sense of wonder , and love and respect , for the Pacific Ocean , its ecology , and its diverse peoples : Asian , Pacific , European , indigenous and colonial . <p> As he notes , this is an " Ocean of the Future ' ' . <p> And we are very much part of it . <p> * Victor Billot is editor of The Maritimes , the magazine of the Maritime Union .

@@4907719 <h> Opinion : Nurturing nature pivotal to climate change response <p> While climate change talks took place in Paris last week , Beijiing was on ' red alert ' for its highest-recorded smog levels . <p> The Paris climate change talks bring to the fore the connectedness of the world we now live in . Climate change is an upfront example of what happens when ecosystems cease to function the way they should . <p> The consequences for humanity are severe and can be linked to mass migration and social unrest . People depend on ecosystems - defined as where all living things , from plants and animals to organisms , share an environment . Overcoming the short-sighted consequences of a free market global economy , we need to invest in what ecological economists call ' natural capital ' in the same way as we invest in other forms of capital , if we are to continue to receive the essential , life-giving dividends from ecosystems . <p> Investment in protecting and maintaining healthy ecosystems gives us ' ecosystem services ' - the benefits that people derive from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Things like clean drinking water and air , pollination of crops , waste decomposition and nutrient cycles . Climate regulation is one ' ecosystem service ' that a healthy , global , shared environment provides . <p> Awareness of the life-support services that ecosystems provide is increasing . In preparation for COP21 , I have been part of the Oceans and Climate Platform and UNESCO 's World Oceans Day . This group of scientists and diplomats is calling attention to the crucial role of oceans in climate regulation , reinforced by the fact that 72 per cent of the earth is covered by oceans . <p> Due to sea level rise , this percentage is growing within our life time . A summary of the first United Nations World Oceans Assessment was recently presented to the United Nations General Assembly . It concluded that " the world is running out of time to bring the overall human impacts on the ocean under sustainable management " . <p> Ecosystem services thinking going mainstream <p> There are hopeful signs that ecosystem services thinking is entering the mainstream . Britain has completed a Natural @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ its thinking . President Obama recently released a memo to " incorporate ecosystem services into federal decision making " . All US federal departments have to consider natural capital and take all ecosystem services into account in policy and decisions to guide them in developing more effective and efficient solutions . <p> New Zealand is certainly on the right track with Treasury 's living standards , by systemically interlinking the core components of well-being which we derive from natural , social , human and built capital . The natural resource sector has been collaborating across the various ministries and departments to consider how to capture the value of nature and use an ecosystem services approach . The challenge seems to be how to overcome the politics and differentiation between value and price . Value is n't the same as price . For example , I value my children , but they are not for sale . Markets function based on price . <p> Yet an unwavering belief in the ability of neoliberal markets and financial systems to rescue the situation does not acknowledge it is part of the problem . We @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ subsidising the industries that contribute to the problem . <p> Pollution threat to environment and humans highlighted in China <p> Nowhere is the issue more frighteningly apparent than in China where , right now , 22.5 million people in Beijing are for the first time on " red alert " - the highest indicator for smog levels . The natural capital that provides them with the ecosystem service of breathable air is profoundly overburdened . It 's been estimated that around 1.4 million Chinese people die prematurely every year due to pollution , much of it the result of manufacturing cheap goods we buy here . This will be further enabled by dysfunctional agreements such as the Trans-Pacific Partnership Agreement , under the banner of ' free markets ' . While New Zealand is far from experiencing such a catastrophe , we are on the same planet , part of the same global ecosystem . <p> We do , however , live in an exciting time , pregnant with opportunity to navigate the paradoxes caused by climate change and related challenges . Many of the pieces for better decision-making are already @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ together . A more coordinated approach to bring together what is already known - a synthesis , in other words - will accelerate progress in adopting more sustainable solutions . <p> Whether we live in Beijng , Beirut , Barcelona or the Bay of Plenty - we 're all bound by a common need to look after the ecosystems that give us life . It 's just good economics . <p> - Associate Professor Marjan van den Belt is Director of Ecological Economics Research New Zealand , based in the College of Humanities and Social Sciences at Massey University 's Manawat ? ? campus .

Photo by Reuters . <p> The climate deal almost 200 countries agreed to in Paris at the weekend was far better than most insiders dared to hope even a month ago . <p> The biggest emitters of greenhouse gases , China and the United States , are finally on board . <p> There is real money on the table to help poor countries cut their emissions and cope with warming . <p> They have even adopted a target of holding the warming to only +1.5degC , instead of the limit of +2degC , the goal when the conference opened . <p> So the several thousand delegates who spent two weeks dickering over the details of the deal in a draughty exhibition hall north of Paris felt fully justified in cheering and congratulating one another on a job well done . <p> Given all that , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the warming . <p> The +2degC limit was always too high . <p> It began as a scientific estimate of when natural feedbacks , triggered by the warming human beings had caused , take over and started driving the temperature much , much higher . <p> It was actually quite a fuzzy number : at somewhere between +1.75degC and +2.25degC , the feedbacks will kick in and it will be Game Over . <p> So +2degC , for political purposes , became the limit . <p> Beyond that , governments told us , we would have " dangerous warming ' ' . <p> Nonsense . <p> We are having dangerous warming now - bigger storms , worse floods , longer droughts - and we are only at +1degC . <p> At +2degC , or thereabouts , what we get is catastrophe : runaway warming that can no longer be halted just by stopping human emissions of carbon dioxide . <p> Nature will take over , and we will be trapped on a one-way escalator taking us up to +3 , +4 , +5 , even +6degC . <p> Hundreds of millions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ large parts of the planet ceased to be habitable by human beings . <p> If you do n't want to risk unleashing that , then you do n't want to go anywhere near +2degC , so the official adoption by the world 's governments of +1.5degC as the never-exceed limit is a major step forward . <p> But note that they have only pledged " to pursue efforts to limit the temperature increase to 1.5C ' ' , not to succeed . <p> The hard-and-fast promise is still not to go past +2degC - and there is not even any guarantee that will be achieved . <p> In order to avoid a collapse like the one at the last climate summit in Copenhagen six years ago , nobody even tried to put enforceable limits on national carbon dioxide emissions this time . <p> Each country was just invited to submit the emission cuts it was willing to make . <p> The sum of all those promised cuts ( if the promises are kept ) is what we will get by way of global emission cuts in the next five years . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ emission cuts fall far short of what is needed . <p> If this is all that is done , then we are headed for at least +2.7degC - or rather , for a lot more , because of the feedbacks . <p> None of the negotiations at the Paris conference changed those numbers , or even tried to . <p> So are we doomed to runaway warming ? <p> Not necessarily . <p> Most of the negotiators know the cuts which are politically impossible now may become quite possible in five or 10 years if the cost of renewable energy goes on dropping , if techniques like carbon capture and sequestration ( CCS ) become economically viable - and if people are sufficiently frightened by a climate that is getting wilder and less predictable by the year . <p> So there is a review process built into the treaty . <p> Every five years , starting in 2018 , there will be a " stock-taking ' ' exercise in which everybody 's progress in cutting their emissions will be reviewed , and everybody will be encouraged to increase their commitments and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ do that depends on political , economic and technological factors that can not yet be calculated , but fear is a great incentive , and there is no government on the planet that is not frightened by the prospect of major climate change . <p> In fact , most of them would have gone a lot further in Paris if they were not nervous about getting too far ahead of public opinion at home . <p> Public opinion will eventually change , because there is going to be a very large amount of damage and suffering in the world as we move past +1degC and head up towards +1.5degC . <p> Will it change fast enough to allow governments to act decisively and in time ? <p> Nobody knows . <p> Will new green technologies simply sweep the field , making fossil fuels uneconomic and government intervention unnecessary ? <p> Nobody knows that either , although many people pin their hopes on it . <p> We are not out of the woods yet , but we are probably heading in the right direction - and it would be right at this @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ maligned organisation , the United Nations . <p> It is the only arena in which global negotiations like this can be conducted , and its skills , traditions and people were indispensable in leading them to a more or less successful conclusion . <p> Although I agree with most of this , it is my view that the public are well ahead of governments on this issue . I managed to get to the last few days of the Climate Conference and although I was not involved in the main discussion , the public area at the back of the main conference hall had seminars going continually from 9am to 6pm . I went to several of them and the only presenters who thought that we did not need to make immediate changes were those sponsored by the energy industries via the Carnegie fund . One presenter involved in the Carbon Sequestration discussion at European Government level was particularly discouraged at what he thought were continual discussions between the same people that were leading nowhere . It is worth pointing out that Carbon Seqestration is far less than 1% of total @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Sequestration compared to 50 gigatons in Carbon emissions yearly ) . There were as the just mentioned presenter said 20 Carbon Sequestration projects that were supposed to be finalized in the European Community by 2020 and he was n't sure that one would actually materialize . The only project mentioned was a Sequestration project in Canada that has gone ahead with the builders saying that they had learnt enough from the project to decrease costs by 40% or so on the next one . It 's not the general public that is stopping action on Climate Change in my view , it 's big energy . And big energy is having too much impact on governments . There are business opportunities in such things as CCS and alternative energy projects , so we should not allow these to be shut down by corporations who have interests in Carbon energy as that is ultimately bad for everyone , even the people who have financial interests in such corporations . 
@@4909747 <h> Firm foundations or hot air ? <p> From the hugs , handshakes , huge applause and standing ovation at the conclusion of the United Nations Climate Change Conference near Paris , onlookers could be forgiven for thinking world leaders and delegates had already saved the planet . <p> The conference 's achievements are certainly notable . <p> The global agreement - by 195 countries and some 20 years in the making - has been described as a " landmark ' ' in terms of setting the future course for climate-change action . <p> Just as remarkable was the vision of a truly united UN . <p> Effective action by the organisation is so often stymied because of the veto rights of the five permanent Security Council members . <p> That all its member states could come to an agreement about something as vast and divisive as climate change is certainly ground- breaking . <p> But does the agreement lay the first much-needed firm foundations or does it amount to a lot of hot air ? <p> The accord lays out a common vision and ambitious plan to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to global warming , and even hopes to keep warming below 1.5degC - even better than the 2degC threshold beyond which scientists say global warming becomes catastrophic and irreversible and which was the initial target at the conference . <p> Rich nations will help poorer ones in the transition to clean energy and to cope with the impacts of climate change . <p> There will be a five-yearly stocktake of countries ' progress on their emissions reductions pledges . <p> The first will be in 2018 , and the first under the official agreement in 2023 . <p> The bad news ? Significant parts of the agreement are not legally binding , and rely on individual countries to make good on their reduction pledges . <p> The world is already 1degC warmer than pre-industrial temperatures . <p> And the current pledges are n't actually enough to keep us within the 2degC threshold . <p> There are many challenges for some of the biggest polluters . <p> The Republican-dominated Congress could block changes in the US . <p> China 's reduction plans are long-term , and India will not wean itself off @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ - although a minnow in overall emissions - is still largely dragging the chain , despite talking the talk on the shape of the agreement and what other nations should be doing around the likes of fossil-fuel subsidies . <p> Outgoing Climate Change Minister Tim Groser said there would be no changes to domestic policy around the issue . <p> Agriculture ( our biggest emitter ) will be left unchecked until such time as science and technology can provide an animal emissions solution , and we will be one of the few nations buying carbon credits from others who are reducing their emissions . <p> On the positive side , the Government has agreed to fund Pacific nations to the tune of $200 million over the next four years . <p> But we must do more than just support others or demand action of them . <p> All countries are concerned about their economic prosperity , all have problems transitioning , but all must be part of the solution . <p> Funding science and technology is certainly desirable , but without real political will and the implementation of meaningful policies , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ achieve changes at the speed necessary . <p> If agriculture really is our hardest issue to " solve ' ' , we should be stepping up efforts in the energy and transport sectors at the very least . <p> And if we wo n't lead - which seems a shame given the clean , green environmentally friendly image we like to project - we must follow or forever be shamed . <p> New Climate Change Minister Paula Bennett needs to put her foot down ( in her electric car , of course ) and start driving home some real domestic policies here so our first stocktake report card is not the woeful one it looks likely to shape up to be .

@@4912735 <h> Q&amp;A : climate change agreement and what it means for farming <p> Dr Rick Pridmore , chairman of the Pastoral Greenhouse Gas Research consortium , says the push for cleaner water will help drive lower emissions . <p> The Paris climate change agreement has been signed . What will it mean for New Zealand farmers and the primary sector ? This Q&amp;A clarifies some points . <p> Q : Are New Zealand farmers big producers of climate change emissions ? <p> A : First of all the big picture : the world 's population is too great and it 's growing ( 7.3 billion today , projected to reach 10 billion by 2050 ) . Until we get around to reducing our numbers , little will change . Secondly , 85 per cent of the world 's problems are industrial emissions - too many cars and dirty factories . <p> DAVID UNWIN / FAIRFAX NZ <p> A cow has its emissions recorded at Massey University 's labs . <p> Q : OK , what part do our farmers play ? <p> A : On a global scale @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cent of the world 's milk , for example . But our cows , sheep , deer , goats and other ruminant livestock burp and fart methane . When other global warming gases are added , agriculture is responsible for 47 per cent of New Zealand 's emissions . It 's not a good look . <p> Q : Are any other countries ' agricultural emissions as high as a proportion of their total emissions ? <p> A : The only developed country which comes close to New Zealand is Ireland , with 32 per cent . But many developing countries are greater or on a par with New Zealand ( Uruguay with 85 per cent emissions from livestock ) . <p> Q : Federated Farmers says New Zealand farmers are very efficient . Is that true ? <p> A : Since 1990 the agricultural sector has made greenhouse gas efficiency gains of about 1 per cent a year . That means they are producing 1 per cent fewer greenhouse gas emissions to produce the same amount of meat , milk and wool . <p> A : Mainly through an intense @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ : Even so , have n't the actual amount of emissions risen since 1990 ? <p> A : Yes , as more farms have converted to dairy , methane emissions have increased 10 per cent . <p> Q : What will force change on farmers ? <p> A : There are several outside forces which will have a big impact in the next decade . The first is the nationwide push for cleaner freshwater . Dr Rick Pridmore , chairman of the Pastoral Greenhouse Gas Research consortium ( PGGRC ) , says in some regions farmers will have to reduce nitrogen to curb pollution . This in turn will mean fewer nitrous oxide emissions . In Hinds , Canterbury , greenhouse gas emissions could be reduced by 30 per cent . Nationwide , the gains will be fewer , but significant . <p> Q : How soon will this happen ? <p> A : By 2017 , " good practice " should lower emissions by 7 per cent . As regulations kick in , there will be greater reductions after 2020 . <p> Q : What else might happen ? <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that milk prices may not go as high as they did two seasons ago . They have had to reduce livestock and inputs . <p> Q : Have they really ? What about palm kernel imports ? <p> A : Greenpeace has attacked Fonterra for contributing to rainforest destruction by feeding cows palm kernel expellar ( PKE ) , a by-product of the palm oil industry . Pridmore says it 's not a simple question . Cows fed on PKE produce less nitrogen and therefore fewer nitrous oxide emissions than if they are fed on grass . But if people buy the argument that demand for PKE fuels rainforest destruction , thereby boosting carbon dioxide emissions , then the dairy industry loses the argument . <p> Q : What other developments are in the wind ? <p> A : Mark Aspin , manager of the PGGRC , predicts approaches currently being developed have the potential to give a further 1.5 per cent a year efficiency gains . These include breeding livestock for lower emissions , low greenhouse gas feeds , development of a methane vaccine and a drench to inhibit methane @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ A : Some brassicas ( rape , swedes ) are being bred which lower emissions , by as much as 30 per cent when fed as a full diet . The problem is that livestock do not live on brassicas 12 months a year , so nationally over a year the reduction might be less than 2 per cent . <p> Q : What about the vaccine and drench ? <p> A : Both could inhibit methane . Pridmore prefers the vaccine because it uses antibodies already produced by the cow . Both it and the drench are at " proof of concept " stage , and could be ready for use in five to seven years . Both technologies are targeting about a 20 per cent cut in emissions .

@@4912823 <p> by Paul Bruce and Sue Kedgley As people all over the world celebrate the fact that 195 nations have committed to drastically reducing their emissions over the next few decades , it 's important that local government , along with everyone else , does whatever it can to reduce emissions in our region . <p> Transport emissions are the most significant source of emissions in the Wellington region ( 37% ) , so reducing carbon emissions from transport should be a high priority for the Regional Council . <p> That 's why we have been campaigning for two years to keep our fleet of 60 non-polluting , zero-emission electric trolley buses ( which have 10-15 years of life left in them ) , and we are deeply disappointed that the rest of the Regional Council is still calling for their demise and replacement , for the next decade at least , with polluting diesel buses . <p> Unless other Regional Councillors can be persuaded to change their mind , our trolley buses will be put out of service from June 2017 , and replaced by diesel buses @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of 400 diesel buses in our region , all increasing carbon emissions and emitting carcinogenic particulates that are a serious public health risk . <p> New diesel buses will be expected to meet a Euro 5 standard ( not even Euro 6 which is the mandated European standard ) . But the existing fleet of around 200 Euro 3 diesel buses will continue to chug around Wellington until they are 20 years old , spewing their diesel fumes . <p> A battery electric bus is to be trialled next year , along with a diesel electric hybrid bus . And the intention is that when electric battery buses become widely available and cost effective , the Council will switch to electric battery buses . The problem is that electric battery buses are heavy and may be limited to 45 passengers , when just relying on overnight charging . <p> This makes them well suited to some bus routes , but not to major bus routes that require high capacity buses , which need to carry at least 75 passengers . By contrast , trolley buses are ideally suited for Wellington 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ complement of batteries and are therefore not so heavy . <p> The Regional Council also intends to introduce a fleet of double decker buses on several routes in the region , but these buses will be so heavy they will exceed allowable weight limits on Wellington roads , which will therefore require strengthening , at some cost . <p> The news about hybrid buses is not good . New York and Toronto ditched them because they did not prove cost effective and the batteries needed replacing too frequently . And three cities in Australia have ditched hybrid buses after trialling them for some time . <p> Despite our frustrations over the new bus fleet in Wellington , we have had some successes . <p> * Sue Kedgley chaired a Climate Change Working group that consulted widely with the community and developed an excellent Climate Change Strategy and Implementation Plan , which the Regional Council has adopted . Paul was an active member of the working group . *She is a member of the Wellington City Council Climate Change Strategy Committee , and is helping to coordinate an Electric Vehicle Symposium that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , to examine the latest electric bus and car technology , and what policy changes are needed to increase the uptake of electric buses and cars in the Wellington region . * The Council agreed to adopt her proposal that it promote and coordinate a network of fast charging stations for electric cars around the region , so that people with electric cars can travel freely in the Wellington region , without fear of running out of power . ( At present there is only one charging station in the region ) . The Council has also agreed to her motion that it replace its fleet of vehicles with electric vehicles , once there are appropriate vehicles available and sufficient charging stations in the region . *She is also Deputy Chair of the recently established Sustainable Transport committee and Paul is a member . * Paul Bruce is the Waste Spokesperson for the Wellington Regional Council and a member of the Regional Waste Forum . He successfully persuaded Local Government New Zealand to agree to a remit calling for all e-waste to have a fee incorporated in their purchase price that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Paul has been urging Local Government NZ to be more active in lobbying the government to implement waste stewardship. ** The Council recently adopted Paul 's motion calling for the Council and Local Government NZ to conduct an analysis of the impacts of the Trans Pacific Partnership Agreement on local government . @@4912934 <h> Awanui flood maps to be released <p> The Awanui-Kaitaia area is poised to become the latest part of Northland to be issued with new maps showing the impacts of a ' one in a 100 year ' flood . <p> The new maps - developed over the past several years - form part of the Northland Regional Council 's Priority Rivers Programme , which focusses on reducing flood risks in 26 Northland catchments at highest threat , including 17 in the Far North . <p> The council 's Te Hiku constituency representative , Monty Knight , says staff will be writing to the owners of about 2600 affected properties shortly detailing the updated flood risk to their properties and directing them to the new maps . <p> He says the council has already released flood maps for 24 catchments over the past three years to raise awareness of where flood hazard areas are and to help inform both public and district council decision-making . <p> " It will probably come as no surprise to locals to hear the Awanui catchment has been one of the most challenging @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " <p> These include the way floodwaters flow over the State Highway and into a different catchment ; the Tarawhataroa Stream . " Flow through the Kaitaia itself is also heavily influenced by the Whangatane Spillway on the north side of town , which typically diverts roughly two-thirds of flood flow from the Awanui River . " <p> Councillor Knight says the new models cost about $250,000 to develop and forecast not only the predicted impacts of a ' 100 year flood ' , but also incorporate Central Government climate change projections on storm rainfall intensity and sea level rise . <p> " There 's a one percent probability of a flood of this scale occurring in any given year . " <p> He says most people living in the Kaitaia area are already all-too-familiar with heavy rainfall events and how these can affect local properties . <p> " Most of the areas covered by our new flood maps have previously been identified as ' flood-susceptible ' and are already shown as such in the Far North District Plan . " <p> However , the new Awanui flood maps do incorporate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ latest available storm data from the National Institute of Water and Atmospheric Research ( NIWA ) . <p> They show that should a 100 year event hit Kaitaia , the worst affected areas would be within known flood-prone areas . <p> The largest flood in recent times occurred in July 2007 and resulted in over-topping of the Tarawhataroa Stream stop bank , flooding adjacent low-lying areas . " <p> Councillor Knight says the new maps will progressively replace previous hazard maps which were based on soil type and topographical features indicating they had suffered heavy flooding in the past . <p> They would also complement a range of other extensive work the regional council has planned to reduce flood risk in the catchment . This includes a $600,000 project to modify the Whangatane spillway intake due to start next year .

Agreement on climate is historic but it requires nations to act and the big companies and investors of the energy sector to get behind it . <p> The deal means countries are likely to continue pursuing climate change targets , which will require a transformation of economies away from fossil fuels to green energy sources . That @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by 2030 , where homes will have to stop relying on gas for heating , and where consumers will continue to pay rising subsidies for renewable electricity through energy bills . <p> " Climate change will affect many sectors and food production will be one of these critical areas -- planning for climate change will be a challenge in our food and agricultural production industry not only for changes in extreme weather conditions such as drought and floods but also acknowledging that our biggest contributor to greenhouse gases is the agricultural sector , " Julyan says . <p> " As a key contributor to our export market and the mainstay of our economy , how will we manage our commitments while maintaining economic wellbeing ? We will be challenged in our businesses and industry to be smart and innovative in meeting our commitments while maintaining our competitiveness and achieving economic success . <p> " Good planning and forethought is going to be so important to New Zealand to maintaining a healthy economy and a sustainable future . Severe weather events are likely to be more apparent and long term issues like sea level rise may threaten coastal areas and so planning our land uses , locating activities and anticipating hazards need to be considered and existing development may need to be reconsidered . <p> " The implications of these have to be faced and planning for change needs to occur as early as possible to avoid future risks or catastrophes . <p> " Maintaining our independence from less sustainable resources which contribute to greenhouse gas emissions is important and New Zealand is fortunate in that we have strong renewable energy sources such as solar , hydro , wind or thermal . Planning for our energy needs into the future will need to continue to be sustainable and clean , " Julyan says .

@@4915721 <h> An Insider 's Account of the Long , Strange Journey to the Paris Climate Accord <p> Kathy Cumming attended the Paris Climate talks as part of the New Zealand delegation , and as media ambassador for the Green party . She writes about witnessing the mundane and magical creation of history . <p> Let me start at the end ; now commonly referred to as " only the beginning " . A deal to prevent climatic catastrophe had been struck on the outskirts of Paris . <p> At 8pm on the evening of December 12th , the French President of the COP21 climate talks -- a dapper , no-nonsense and frankly heroic Laurent Fabius -- whacked his gavel down to mark the miracle . C'est fait . Bon nuit fossil fuels . <p> For sure people cried . Many among the 45,000 people there had dedicated decades to getting this thing out of the blocks . A colleague and I sat with just our shoulders touching , tears dropping quickly and quietly onto our laps . <p> Paris became synonymous with two things at the tail end @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ conversely , was the saving of the world . COP21 . The last chance saloon . As in , seriously now guys , we either do it this time or Devonport 's an island and Tuvalu a former one . <p> The security risk in Paris sat stubbornly on " extreme " . Metro stations had been evacuated in the days leading up to the talks due to bomb scares . A security advisor at Parliament called me up for a personal safety briefing . " When in Gay Paree .. ! " he quipped . He assured me the risk of being pick-pocketed remained exponentially higher than being taken out in a mass shooting . <p> As I packed my French phrasebook and thick coat , Bill English dismissed projected sea level rise as " speculative " , and the Prime Minister said science would save us and excluded agriculture from the ETS review . <p> I set off for France , full of hope for a new dawn . <p> Image courtesy of @davidtong <p> You travel . You tip time on its head . And it fights back @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Frankfurt . You have to fly high above the clouds to find light in Europe these days . ( I noticed the metaphor in that only after thinking it ; mostly I was just bracing for winter ) . <p> Paris smelt more like piss than fear . People were doing what the media said they were doing -- " getting on with things " . <p> Day One of COP ( standing for " Conference of the Parties to the UN Convention on Climate Change " ) . Giant warehouses and elaborate tents had been erected on an 18 hectare exhibition site called Le Bourget , 15 km from Paris . There the suits and scarves had set up camp . It was a travelling circus , a small city , and our home for the next two weeks . <p> Obama arrived . Round the back . He said all the right things -- " I actually think we 're going to do it . I actually think we 're going to solve this thing " . Trudeau was cavalier -- " Canada is back ! " <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ halls , countries ' plaques on desks and spindly microphones at the ready for words of diplomacy , obstruction , compromise , despair . <p> UN climate chief Christiana Figueres -- dapper , no-nonsense and frankly heroic -- said " never has a responsibility so great been in the hands of so few " . Was it comforting to know Tim Groser was among the few ? That is a question I asked myself . <p> John Key rocked up . He was jovial ; attempting a gentle gag on the world stage which went down okay-if-not-great . He spoke of " courage and political will " on climate change . Say what ? He called for an end to fossil fuel subsidies worldwide , neglecting to mention the tens of millions of taxpayer dollars he chucks at oil and gas exploration every year . This audacious display of hypocrisy earned New Zealand COP 's first Fossil of the Day Award . What I most liked about the subsidy event was its hashtag -- #endFFS . <p> Within the vast perimeter of the conference site , the baguette queue hardly moved @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the lunch queue next to a man from Gambia . His soup and lentils and bread came to 14 euros . " I can not afford that , " he said , and put it all back . <p> We were in Europe of course , so there were some terrifically attractive people about . It was enough to make you self-reflective . As a fellow New Zealander said to me , " we 're good people , but let 's face it , we 're not very good looking " . <p> Then there were the celebrities : Sean Penn , Arnold Schwarzenegger , John Kerry , Al Gore , Barack , Richard Branson , Lord Nicholas Stern . Who the hell was n't at this thing ? 3,000 of the world 's journalists sure were . Double that number had applied for accreditation . From New Zealand ? Chris Bramwell of RNZ and the Herald 's Jamie Morton cut lonely Kiwi figures ( note well that Jamie was funded not by his publication , but jointly by the Science Media Centre and Morgan Foundation ) . They worked like @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ early hours for Checkpoint . Her antisocial schedule made the BBC . <p> " We make a proposal to remove the brackets . " " Can we remove the brackets ? " " Any objections to removing the brackets ? There is an objection . We continue the discussion " " We 'd like to propose new brackets " " Can I suggest we do n't add any more brackets lest the whole thing becomes bracketed ? " This planet-saving business seemed to boil down to pairs of square marks at the beginning and the end of sentences . Who knew ? <p> One US negotiator had a perpetually tense jaw . " I 'm not trying to waste time , I 'm actually trying to save time , " he told the Chair as they battled over ... you guessed it , brackets . <p> It was nice being able to put faces to countries . One of China 's negotiators was so young and open-faced ! Surely not the type to knee-cap a decent deal with mulish demands for developed countries to do more . <p> Brackets were inserted @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ) Brazil likened them to " carbon molecules ... polluting our text " . She said " it 's possible that our puffed up prideful intelligence has outstripped our instinct for survival . " People really were discussing parts per million in the loos . You could not knock these 45,000 people who 'd come from all corners of the globe . They were full spectrum , knee-deep in climate . An updated text , down from 46 pages to 26 and -- wait for it -- a 75% reduction @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to work through the night . Many were adamant this whole thing was going to be a cop out . <p> Back at Le Bourget we bit our nails and milled around . She was ratified . 195 countries signed up to the first ever universal agreement on climate change . <p> A gamechanger , historic , momentous , a turning point , potentially bigger than the industrial revolution .
