@@4915787 <p> But then came Monday , and the gargantuan scale of the economic and cultural shift that is required came back into focus . As former New Zealand climate change ambassador Adrian Macey noted in the cold light of the morning after , the deal itself will not save a single tonne of CO2 ; it 's all about what countries , businesses and individuals do to break our reliance on fossil fuels . <p> The Paris agreement has been described by one observer as a " collection of aspirations , some of them contradictory " . It seeks to hold warming as close as possible to 1.5 ? C , yet it is underpinned by carbon-reduction pledges that set the world on a path to 2.7 ? C of warming . It requires the world to get to zero net emissions , but the global economy relies on fossil fuels for 80% of its energy . <p> Yet , despite the vagueness and unfinished business , this is a powerful agreement because it gives us a clear , globally agreed target . That means investors in clean @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ certainty and confidence . It establishes a framework for countries to reach for the increasingly ambitious emission reductions that will be essential . And it forms a promise against which people will be able to hold their governments to account . <p> That includes the New Zealand Government , which has justifiably come under pressure to defend its climate policies . It has pledged an 11% reduction in emissions compared with 1990 levels , yet we are already more than 20% above 1990 and rising . By the Government 's own account , current climate policies -- of which the emissions trading scheme ( ETS ) is the centrepiece -- will have almost no effect on emissions , and the only way we will meet our obligations is by buying overseas carbon credits . <p> The ETS itself has been the subject of ridicule for years , with participants able to trade in meaningless " hot air " credits , and the price of carbon dropping at one point as low as 20c . This in turn has caused a collapse in new forest planting . <p> And barely four days @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of new oil and gas exploration permits , raising the question whether it makes strategic sense to be promoting a sector that is facing demise if the world is to reach zero carbon emissions . <p> But there is also the potential for positive change , and soon . The scheme is being reviewed ( but without our major emitting industry , agriculture ) , and the pressure will be on the Government to turn its present rickety framework into a much tighter regime with stable rules , a finite carbon currency and a floor price . In other words , a scheme capable of steering investment into low-carbon technologies . <p> And with transport emissions making up 20% of our total , attention is rightly turning to the enormous potential for electric vehicles . With our predominantly renewable electricity supply and stable national grid , New Zealand is a sitter for EVs . ? Transport Minister Simon Bridges understands this and is ? promising measures in 2016 to increase their use . <p> But decarbonising the economy is not just about government policy . It 's also about us and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with cars carrying one person ; our children are driven to school rather than walking or biking ; international travel has become a middle-class expectation rather than a rare and cherished experience ; our measure of social success is a beach house , a boat and a big car to tow it . <p> As individuals and households , we can lighten our footprint . Some already do . Research by economic group Motu shows that some households produce half the volume of emissions as others with the same income , because they drive and fly less and eat less red meat and dairy products . Many of our emissions are the product of habit -- we get in the car to go and buy a loaf of bread , for instance , because it does n't occur to us to bike to the shop . <p> But we are capable of deep cultural change . Remember when it was okay to smoke at work and in restaurants , or to get behind the wheel after a few drinks ? Kicking the carbon habit calls for the same kind of shift @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ humankind 's greatest ? challenge . As Victoria University 's David Frame puts it , " The more you burn , the warmer you get . " To turn down the heat , every action will count . 
@@4915817 <h> All Blacks ' World Cup success a great example of planning - NZPI <p> The All Blacks ' ultimate success at the Rugby World Cup was one of the best examples of planning in 2015 , the New Zealand Planning Institute ( NZPI ) says . <p> Proposed changes to the Resource Management Act ( RMA ) , planning discussions on impending sea level rises which could affect many New Zealand cities , more affordable housing and planning for major projects were other major notable issues in 2015 , NZPI chief executive Susan Houston says . <p> NZPI is a fast expanding not for profit key national organisation representing more than 2000 planners all over New Zealand who want New Zealand to be a much better place for future generations because of planning . <p> " All the New Zealand public can step back and realise that the All blacks ' world cup success has been the result of four years ' of planning . @@4915996 <h> More than 16m babies born into conflict this year - UNICEF <p> More than 16 million babies were born in conflict zones in 2015 - 1 in 8 of all births worldwide this year - UNICEF said today , a figure that underscores the vulnerability faced by increasing numbers of children . <p> " Every two seconds , a newborn takes its first breath in the midst of conflict , often in terrifying circumstances and without access to medical care , " said UNICEF Executive Director Anthony Lake . " Too many children are now starting their lives in extreme circumstances - from conflict to natural disasters , poverty , disease or malnutrition . Can there be a worse start in life ? " <p> In conflict-affected countries such as Afghanistan , the Central African Republic , Iraq , South Sudan , Syria and Yemen , or on perilous journeys to escape fighting , newborn children and their mothers face enormous risks . Pregnant mothers are in danger of giving birth without medical help and in unsanitary conditions . Their children are more likely to die before @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ or " toxic " - stress , which can inhibit their long-term emotional and cognitive development . <p> In addition to conflict , poverty , the effects of climate change and lack of opportunity are making children increasingly vulnerable and have pushed millions on dangerous journeys away from their homes . <p> - More than 200,000 children applied for asylum in European Union countries in the first nine months of 2015 , adding to the 30 million children across the globe forced from their homes by 2014 due to war , violence and persecution . More people are displaced now than at any moment since World War II . <p> - More than a quarter of a billion children - or 1 in 9 - live in countries and areas in conflict and face enormous obstacles to their health , education and well-being . <p> - More than half a billion children live in areas where floods are extremely common and nearly 160 million live in high or extremely high drought severity zones . <p> - Children represent almost half of all people living in extreme poverty although they make up @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " The final months of 2015 have seen the world come together around tackling climate change and a new global development agenda . These ambitious agreements present a huge opportunity if we can translate our promises into action for the most vulnerable children , " said Lake . " If we address the reasons so many families feel the need to uproot themselves and their children from their homes - by resolving conflict , by addressing climate change , by expanding opportunity , we can make 2016 a year of hope for millions - not a year of despair . "

She can hardly get her head straight let alone getting it around someone else 's world and words . @@4918595 <h> Ross Henderson : On climate change and Keytruda <p> OPINION : It was a great week for the government . Which seems a bit convenient . <p> Looking at the fine print -- which even the Green Party and our local environment crews seem to have missed -- it seems that a lot of the most important things we need to do to address climate change , pledging to curb carbon emissions , are n't legally binding in any way . <p> When you do n't get punished for inaction , why would you take action ? <p> The good news is that our government has finally figured out that climate change is real -- or at least , enough voters know it 's real that they have to look like they 're doing something . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ headlines about this great Paris climate change deal , they probably think they 've done enough . <p> But climate change is real . And even if it 's not directly threatening you or I right now , it will . <p> We only have to look at our Pacific neighbours who will very quickly be looking for somewhere else to live with even a tiny rise in sea levels . We 're a hospitable country , but we do n't want hundreds or thousands of climate refugees desperately hammering at the door -- for their sake as well as ours . <p> I 'm not advocating any extreme greenie policies about shutting down every mine and oil rig immediately and eating nothing but tofu for the rest of our lives -- this would be chaos , pulling the plug on vital , often well-paid , jobs which sustain their communities . <p> But the oil and coal are n't going to last forever , and our planet needs us to find different ways of living which do n't involve cooking the atmosphere . We need a plan to ensure our @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have a future -- because climate change threatens all of that even if it is n't obvious to us now . <p> Unfortunately , now they 've got a bit of paper to wave around , I suspect our government will just keep on pretending everything 's hunky-dory . <p> While we 're talking about our government 's love of talking big to hide its lack of action , my comrade John Sargeant had a bit to say about Labour 's support for the melanoma drug Keytruda last week , usually , and probably like most of you I stopped reading much of what John has to share . However last week I made an exception . <p> His mathematical argument is sound on face value -- if you fund one thing , you ca n't fund another -- but that ignores the real point of Labour 's campaign . It 's not just about this one drug , which could save many of the hundreds of people killed by melanoma every year in New Zealand . <p> It 's about the way this government has chronically underfunded our health system @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have made a huge song and dance about increasing healthcare funding at more than the rate of inflation . It 's a bit of an improvement , sure . But it 's done nothing to make up for the effective funding cuts over their first five years in office , and it ignores the inescapable reality that our population is getting older . Our healthcare needs are increasing . <p> Labour has independent research showing that National has underfunded our healthcare system to the tune of $1.7 billion over the past six years , and short changed Pharmac by $115 million . <p> And let 's not forget that once we 're fully committed to the TPP , the costs of many medicines is only going to increase . <p> Pharmac must be allowed to make independent , evidence-based decisions about the medicines they purchase . But that independence is threatened when their funding is squeezed . Penny-pinching starts to take a higher priority . <p> Defenders of the government have no answer to that except the usual pap about tightening our belts and living within our means -- even though @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ I have to wonder if some of the criticism of Labour 's campaign is a sign of nervousness among the right . National did n't just challenge Pharmac -- they bypassed the agency to fund Herceptin directly within weeks of being sworn in . <p> Maybe what 's good for the political goose should be good for the political gander .

we can not find evidence of anywhere else in the world where a commercial scale biofuels plant has been built without a grant or other government incentive such as a mandate or tax incentives . <p> " What has enabled us to be bold enough to invest in this venture has @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , such as Fonterra , who are trying to find ways to reduce their own carbon emissions . <p> " This is an example of New Zealand companies working together to make a difference to our own backyard , " said David . <p> Fonterra 's General Manager of National Transport and Logistics , Barry McColl , said the Co-operative is pleased that its commitment is helping Z to bring this capability to New Zealand while reducing its own emissions . <p> " Fonterra is committed to reducing environmental impacts across all of our operations . Transporting more than 18 billion litres of milk around the country every year requires a lot of hours on the road - our fleet travels more than 90 million kilometres over the year - so we 're hugely supportive of initiatives like this that help to reduce our emissions . " Wildlife does n't stop at an invisible boundary so we need to consider all our oceans and protect our unique biodiversity with more marine reserves , especially in unique areas like the Auckland west coast " . <p> The WRPS calls on the Government to increase @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ EEZ in the marine protection legislation .

@@4926286 <h> Proactive approach succeeds <p> When it comes to grappling with water-quality issues , Madeline Hall has a suggestion for farmers . <p> They need to take a pro-active role to think about rules coming in and ask themselves how they could be involved to help make it work , Ms Hall , an environmental sociology masters student at the University of Otago 's Centre for Sustainability , said . <p> Ms Hall has researched the social impact of a nutrient-reduction nitrogen-trading scheme on farming communities in the Lake Taupo basin . The innovative market-based environmental policy was established to address growing community concern about water quality . <p> She interviewed Taupo farmers earlier this year , returning to the area last month to share her thesis research findings with them . The programme was an experiment and they were the ' ' guinea pigs ' ' for the nation , she said . <p> Under the trial scheme , the amount of nitrogen farms produced was capped using a formula based on production and stock levels . <p> Farmers who could reduce their nitrogen could sell surplus @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in deficit . Additionally , they could sell credits to a third-party group that would retire the credits permanently . <p> Farmers involved were very proud of their actions and they made the ' ' best of a bad situation ' ' . She was also impressed with their active engagement with the regional council . <p> As a result of the negotiation process , farmers and the council were both able to ' ' completely understand ' ' , as well as articulate , the other group 's position . That allowed them to manage the problem in the best possible way for everybody involved . <p> There was no doubt such conversations must be happening across the country , especially with the National Policy Statement for Freshwater Management , and they were hard conversations to have , she said . <p> ' ' These are complex problems . ' ' She believed a lot of faith was put in technology being ' ' our saviour ' ' , and that worried her . <p> While technology would be a fix for some things , there were still issues that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ DC , Ms Hall first came to New Zealand in 2010 as a foreign exchange student and ' ' absolutely fell in love with it ' ' . <p> Her favourite part of the experience was working on a diverse range of farms through the Willing Workers on Organic Farms scheme , working throughout the country . <p> She ' ' absolutely loved it ' ' and worked on a diverse range of properties , including goat , sheep and beef , alpaca and dairy farms , as well as a vineyard . She was particularly interested in conversations on-farm about the emissions trading scheme . <p> Once back in the US , she made it her ' ' mission ' ' to return to New Zealand , applying for a Fulbright scholarship . <p> Being at the Centre of Sustainability was exciting ; it had a wonderful history of talking to farming communities and a good reputation with farmers . Originally intending to research climate change , she discovered that water quality was ' ' on everyone 's mind ' ' . <p> When she was told the story of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Taupo farming community , those experiences caught her ear . <p> Mr and Mrs Barton , who have now been three years farming under a nitrogen cap , sought to increase the value of the product they produced . <p> They sell their beef under the Taupo Beef brand and the business has grown to include five other farmers and has been extended to lamb . It was hoped to eventually sell to an export market . <p> Taupo farmers were very welcoming and helpful as they shared their experiences with her . That included being identified as ' ' polluters ' ' , and how they did not like that . <p> That was something that was very important to remember : the term ' ' polluter ' ' meant something completely different for different groups . <p> Farmers in the catchment were not typical ' ' polluters ' ' ; they did not purposely put effluent in the river or know their actions were having an impact on the lake . Thirty years ago , many farmers were settled in the area by government ballot farm schemes . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ lake water quality was protected , they were told the lake was in trouble and they were to blame . The solution was a cap on nitrogen , which many farmers saw as a cap on farm production . <p> Farmers were now being asked to both ' ' feed the world ' ' and ' ' save the world ' ' at the same time , which could pose financial and practical challenges on farm . <p> Within the catchment , farmers were now telling their story of transition from identifying as producers , to polluters , to nitrogen managers . <p> Although the idea of farming had not really changed , and farming practice had not altered that much , farmers ' bottom line was now centred around kilograms of nitrogen per hectare , not kilograms of meat per hectare . <p> For a long time , farmers had to work directly with the regional council to have them understand their perspective . <p> The entire process , from the discovery of the problem in 1999 through the first stab at a solution in 2008 and the final rules @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , took a total of 16 years . <p> Farmers suggested the council just assumed they could put rules on and ' ' be done with it ' ' . Both parties learned quickly they needed to work collaboratively to come up with a solution that worked for everyone . <p> ' ' In all , we need to to engage directly with farmers when confronting our problem of water quality , ' ' she said . <p> Ms Hall was excited about starting work next year in the climate policy team at the Ministry for Primary Industries in Wellington .

If we are to see a shift we must behave differently . Social enterprises have the ability to move us away from the charity mindset to more self-sufficient organisations , " says Culpan . <p> The report , Social Enterprise Insights Aotearoa , notes that Maori values and enterprise are already aligned with this way of operating and that pockets of social entrepreneurship are thriving around the country . <p> Unlike countries such as the States and Scotland , however , New Zealand does not have an amenable legal framework for social enterprises nor widespread understanding of what they are . <p> " If you ask business , they will assume it 's charity and non-profit organisations often see it as business . <p> " In fact , social enterprises are the best of both worlds and enabling them to register separately , easily receive philanthropic funding @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ says Culpan . <p> Interviewees called on the government to review the conditions to enable social enterprise to thrive in this country . <p> Capability and capital investment were also top of mind for the leaders interviewed with one noting that like small businesses , start up social enterprises are vulnerable . <p> The areas of social impact investing ( funding mechanisms and schemes specifically aimed at helping social entrepreneurs ) and social procurement ( business and government actively seeking suppliers whose profits are invested in addressing social or environmental issues ) were also singled out as focus areas .

subsequently seen the 150-year @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rise ( and still not infrastructure ) for the flooding . <p> In support of his comments , Mr Cull mentions reports and studies in a scatter-gun approach . <p> The result is , in my opinion , a mishmash of personal , unqualified opinion and misinterpretation that should carry little weight but , regrettably , will unnecessarily alarm South Dunedin residents and inevitably depress their property values . <p> Landowners outside this speculative " red zone ' ' may benefit from this . <p> Mr Cull quotes a 3.3mm average annual sea-level rise off Green Island reported in the Otago Regional Council 's report of the June flood , but fails to properly acknowledge the sea-level data for the June event presented in the same document . <p> There were no abnormally high sea levels recorded during the flood , so the mayor 's claim sea-level rise was a factor in this flood seems entirely spurious . <p> Similarly , the regional council report 's data demonstrates bore water levels peaked coincident with high rainfall during the June event , and were thus not controlled by sea levels as stated @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> His further assertion that " we know that just a tiny rise in sea level will cause periodic ponding in parts of the city , whether it rains or not ' ' is both alarmist , ill-considered and not valid for the vast majority even of low-lying South Dunedin . <p> For the record , the rainfall event was of the order of a 20- to 30-year event , depending on what is considered to be the critical rainfall duration . <p> Such an event could normally be expected to exceed stormwater infrastructure capacity and cause surface flooding . <p> Any stormwater infrastructure in poor condition , not working or subject to poor maintenance would increase the impacts of that flooding . <p> Conversation on possible sea-level rise and impacts is to be welcomed , but the conversation needs to be held in a manner that is rational and responsible . <p> Much thanks to a person who is qualified to comment . It is coming from experts or persons previously responsible for infrastructure in the old DCC , which perhaps coincidentally , or maybe not , had little or no debt . <p> Could this have something to do with the many activities this council has got involved with that do not form part of the community 's requirement of local government - that is sewage treatment , water provision , wastewater and local roading ? <p> Could we get our council back to core business only ? He failed completely to examine the various failures in storm water pumping infrastructure but chose to blame something which the DCC were not capable of controlling - and which clearly was not at all to blame . Cull and a few of his Council colleagues have become fixated on climate change ( or as it used to be called Global Warming , or Greenhouse Change ) which , if it is happening , is an enormously complex mixture of natural effects and man-made effects . The changes take place over long periods of time . Cull should be ensuring that Dunedin 's infrastructure ( which is one of the core responsibilities of the Council ) is planned , developed and maintained to a standard capable of meeting entirely forseeable events such as heavy rainfalls . If others wish to become involved in political movements , let them - but Cull and his Council should start to pay attention to what their jobs really are . 
@@4931629 <p> The Paris Agreement on climate change will be at the heart of progress in public health in every country around the world , providing the blueprint for a healthy and safe future . The deal lays the groundwork for global action to limit warming well ... Tuesday 22 December 2015 <p> Paris Agreement is health agreement for the 21st Century <p> The Paris Agreement on climate change will be at the heart of progress in public health in every country around the world , providing the blueprint for a healthy and safe future . The deal lays the groundwork for global action to limit warming well below 2 ? C , and needs to catalyse a transition to a decarbonised economy while protecting human well-being . <p> The agreement has been made possible by a fast-growing global momentum from all parts of society . The global health sector made its voice heard at COP21 , with declarations representing over 1700 health organizations , over 8000 hospitals and health facilities , and 13 million health professionals calling for urgent action to protect our climate and our health . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the actions we now take . Much work needs to be done by the governments of all countries , including New Zealand . The health impacts of climate change are already being seen around the world with hundreds of thousands already dying as a result of climate change each year and millions more affected by the health burden of our carbon-intensive economies . Man-made air pollution alone claims one in eight lives worldwide and more than a thousand deaths per year in New Zealand . <p> Health professionals are calling for governments to build on their progress in Paris . @@4939804 <h> Crystallising an Antarctic strategy <p> MICHAEL WRIGHT <p> Last updated 16:27 , December 26 2015 <p> REUTERS <p> An Adelie penguin stands atop a block of melting ice in the Antarctic . <p> Christchurch has been fighting with itself over how to market its Antarctic gateway status . Now the city is finally putting up a united front - possibly just in time as the world turns towards the poles to understand more about climate change . Michael Wright reports . <p> Some time around 2001 , Sue Stubenvoll noticed something strange . Recently returned from a trip to Antarctica , she found that two of Christchurch 's main attractions promoting the continent -- the International Antarctic Centre and the Canterbury Museum -- did n't stock each other 's brochures . <p> It seemed odd . Surely people interested in one might consider visiting the other ? <p> DAVID WALKER/FAIRFAX NZ <p> Getting down for a tight turn at the NZ Icefest Sled Dog races in North Hagley Park . <p> " They saw each other as competing for the limited time which visitors to Christchurch had @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ each other fine but in their own world their view was ' do n't advertise the other people , they 're competition ' . " <p> So it was that at the turn of the twenty-first century , nearly 100 years after Antarctic pioneers Sir Ernest Shackleton and Robert Falcon Scott saw fit to use the city as their stepping off point to the ice , Christchurch appeared to be in a contest with itself over how best to celebrate the fact . <p> ANNA PEARSON/FAIRFAX NZ <p> Santa is led into the dining room at Scott Base in Antarctica by domestic staff member Heather Pickard , during Christmas celebrations on Sunday . <p> Fifteen years later , the landscape is vastly different . An Antarctic office will be established at the Christchurch City Council next year and the city 's major Antarctic event -- Icefest -- is being overhauled . Plans are afoot to set up an Antarctic research centre in Christchurch and China is showing sufficient interest in the continent to think they might consider an Antarctic programme based in the city . <p> Christchurch had spent most of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Base was established in 1957 and Christchurch was how you got there . Ships docked in Lyttelton for fuel , supplies and servicing and planes flew to the ice from Christchurch International Airport . Antarctica New Zealand managed the logistics and the city eventually hosted the US , Italian and South Korean Antarctic programmes . There was the Antarctic Society , the Antarctic Heritage Trust , the University of Canterbury 's Gateway Antarctica programme -- offering a unique chance to study on the ice -- and the Canterbury Development Corporation ( CDC ) and the city council to do what they could to promote and support Christchurch 's Antarctic pedigree . <p> Still , nothing tied the whole thing together . Christchurch 's two Antarctic shopfronts did n't want their visitors to know the other existed . Stubenvoll , a management consultancy expert with KPMG , saw an opening . <p> Howard O'Kane <p> Tossing Water into Antarctic Air . A photo from Antarctica New Zealand Pictorial Collection , 1961-1962 <p> " We wanted to make sure on the commercial side that we were n't missing out on things , " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ repair programme or servicing programme to Hobart for one of the ships . We wanted to find a way to see what contracts were coming up in advance and get people ready to bid for them . " <p> She approached all the relevant parties then hit up the Christchurch City Council to broker a meeting . They loved the idea , even providing Stubenvoll with a " secretary " to help with the diplomatic side of things . <p> " I 'm inclined to just say what I think , " she said . <p> From then on , Antarctic Link Canterbury ( ALC ) met every few months to discuss how to best serve its members ' common interests . Progress came quickly . <p> " As soon as they started sharing information they saw things that they could do together , " Stubenvoll said . <p> " It was n't just , ' We 've got these big players and they 're going to do wonders ' . It was , ' We 've got all these little people and if we put it together as a package @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ centre , in every sense of the word ' . " <p> The office would act as a liaison between the city 's players in Antarctic science , tourism , environmentalism , logistics and economic development . It would devise a strategy to bring together all the disparate strands in the Antarctic programme to ensure the most was made of every opportunity and to build Christchurch 's gateway profile . <p> " It will develop a co-ordinated approach to Antarctic-related business opportunities and more co-ordinated approach to positioning and marketing Christchurch as the leading gateway city , " Dalziel said . <p> Experts agreed . A Lincoln University report on the economic value of Antarctic-related activities found some stakeholders felt Canterbury took them for granted . <p> " It was suggested Canterbury would benefit from an Antarctic advocacy and co-ordination office to help smooth the various national Antarctic Programme and supplier interactions , to maintain linkages between participants , to assist in negotiations , and to provide a conduit for information . " <p> A feasibility study , delivered to the council , recommended establishing the office and estimated it would @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ more than ever , Canterbury organisations want a new vision and strategy so they can capitalise further on opportunities that surround Antarctica , " it said . <p> Those reports were written in 2007 and 2008 . What happened ? The feasibility study recommended a business plan be developed and an " Antarctic Group " be formed to make the office happen . The city council has no record of a business case or any formal document reaching official channels . <p> Former mayor Garry Moore was a staunch advocate of the office , but ca n't remember why it never came to fruition . Former Antarctica New Zealand chief executive Lou Sanson believes it carried over to the next council before slipping off the radar . " <p> Parker felt New @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ What we probably missed 20 or 30 years ago was the chance to make Christchurch the global Antarctic centre , " he said . <p> " New Zealand . . . could have stood up and done a USA at the United Nations and said , ' Let 's become the nation around which the understanding of research and relationship with Antarctica is centred ' , and we did n't do that . <p> " Ever since then , to a degree , we 've been playing catch up . " <p> A lot of ground has been reclaimed . Antarctica New Zealand -- something of a magnetic pole for all things Antarctica in Canterbury -- runs Scott Base and represents New Zealand on the 30-strong Council of Managers of National Antarctic Programs ( COMNAP ) , liaising with other countries that have science , research , tourism and education interests on the continent . If there was a UN for Antarctica , COMNAP would be it . Christchurch , through the University of Canterbury , recently started a second six-year stint hosting its secretariat . <p> Current Antarctica New Zealand @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ soon after starting in the job in 2014 . <p> " There is an element of contestability and competition but countries still see collaboration above all else . And I see the exactly the same thing with the Antarctic office . " <p> Beggs is philosophical about why the office has n't happened yet . <p> " People change , " he said . <p> " Councillors change , mayors change . Significant things happen to the city , which would have put it lower on the agenda . <p> " Could it have been done sooner ? Probably . But I 'd rather look and say it 's being done now and it has my full support . " <p> So , eight years later everybody still agrees Christchurch needs an Antarctic office and strategy . An advisor will likely be appointed early next year . Beggs is on the appointment panel . The standard of applicants is high , he said . <p> *** <p> Despite the absence of a masterplan , Christchurch has looked to leverage plenty out of its Antarctic links . The Antarctic Festival ran from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 100,000 people attended the first event , which featured public-friendly Antarctic attractions , films , exhibitions and a series of guest speakers . <p> The biennial event was held twice before funding issues arose . The Government contributed $400,000 to the running of both instalments then declared it was not its policy to bankroll individual events indefinitely . <p> There were other problems . An independent report found " a visible level of frustration " among stakeholders at the " absence of a well-thought-through and universally accepted raison d'etre for the festival " . A disconnect between sponsors and many stakeholders meant the latter had unrealistic expectations of what the financial backing would bring . <p> Icefest had an identity crisis , too . With no Antarctic strategy , it had become the de facto replacement , the report said . Something it was not intended or equipped to be . Opportunities were being missed . <p> " Potential exists for Christchurch to take ownership of things Antarctic outside of the Gateway City status , " the report said . <p> Icefest was well placed to benefit from that , it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ consistent , coherent and on-going conversation around why Antarctica is so very special , what relevance that has to a diverse range of audiences , and why anyone in Christchurch should care " . <p> The festival has been abandoned for 2016 while a business plan , sponsorship strategy and budget are drawn up . A smaller event will run in its place to support the 2016 Antarctic summer season , likely with a new name . <p> " Icefest does n't actually say what it is , " Dalziel said , " It 's one of those cutesy phrases that describes a festival of ice . " <p> An Antarctic strategy for the city will help . Give Icefest , or whatever it gets called , direction and a place in the " ultimate celebration of the Antarctic story " , as the report put it . <p> Peter Beggs likes to call it the " joining of the dots " . <p> " Setting up this Antarctic office is absolutely crucial to setting a framework for success in the future , " he said . <p> " Not only @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ conversation from what does Antarctica do for Christchurch to what is the impact that Christchurch can have on Antarctica ? " <p> " That sounds a bit sort of Winston Churchill , does n't it ? <p> It does . But will a rhetorical flourish make us care ? The public will queue for the chance to sit in an American C-17 plane on an open day , but what about the science , research and environmentalism ? The things that make Antarctica really important ? <p> " Our objective . . . is that Antarctica gets into the living room of every Kiwi , " Beggs said . <p> " There are so many things that we learn about Antarctica which is not about penguins , killer whales and big aircraft . " <p> Much of it revolves around climate change . Increasingly , the polar regions are where scientists learn the most about how global warming is changing the planet . <p> " It 's no coincidence that nearly every country in the world is looking to the poles and saying we need to understand what 's happening there @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , " CDC chief executive Tom Hooper said . <p> " Relating the science to what 's going on down at the pole and then bringing that back to Christchurch and saying Christchurch is playing a role in that and there 's all kinds of potential benefits for the city . . . that really no one talks about . " <p> The plan is to achieve this through the Antarctic Endeavour -- a joint research , education and visitor attraction facility . It might sound simplistic , but white lab coats , test tubes and microscopes in a laboratory on Cantabrians ' doorstep would breed a sense of ownership : Christchurch is the place for all things Antarctica . <p> " This would be an international focus for Antarctic research in Christchurch , " Bryan Storey said . <p> " If you think about what 's out at the airport , we have Antarctica New Zealand . . . we have the International Antarctic Centre . If you , just to complete that triangle , had an Antarctic research centre sitting there as well with labs and scientists then you @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the head of Gateway Antarctica , the University of Canterbury 's Antarctic research centre . Its hotly-contested postgraduate programme gives 16 students a year the unique chance to visit Scott Base and study on the ice . <p> A business plan for Antarctic Endeavour is being put together . The research hub would be a huge boost for the university and Christchurch 's Antarctic credentials , Storey said , not just from a science perspective , but PR-wise as well . <p> " We absolutely need to continue to work on that . @@4939806 <h> Significant trade deals benefit NZ <p> New Zealand is again set to benefit from two significant trade announcements made recently , giving hope to the agriculture sector , in particular , for more open access to previously closed markets . <p> The World Trade Organisation reached deals on agricultural export subsides , food aid and other issues last weekend . The weekend of negotiations capped a ministerial conference in the Kenyan capital of Nairobi where rich and poor countries were split over the path of trade reform . <p> While the deal is said to end years of stalemate , concern is now arising about the fate of the poor in countries like India , where the removal of some tariffs and barriers could impose more misery on people scraping a living in factories making products for the Indian domestic market . <p> Newly-appointed Trade Minister Todd McClay is understandably positive about the ongoing effects for New Zealand from the Nairobi agreement and also , the implementation of the New Zealand-Korea free-trade agreement signed by his predecessor Tim Groser . <p> From last Sunday , current duty-free @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' ' and existing tariffs will be eliminated on $793 million , or 48% , of New Zealand exports . <p> The wine industry benefits immediately from a full elimination of the 30% tariff on their exports to Korea - a significant win for wineries in Central Otago , and other wine-growing regions . Also , New Zealand 's cherry exports will benefit from duty-free access , right in the middle of their growing season . The previous tariff on cherry exports was 24% . <p> New Zealand has been a leading voice for the scrapping of subsidies since the economy was radically changed under the leadership of former Labour finance minister Sir Roger Douglas . <p> Tariffs were cut quickly and sharply , causing factories in cities like Dunedin to close as manufacturers looked for cheaper sources of labour . <p> Dunedin has continued to see its heavy manufacturing base decline as imports from China have taken over functions of the former thriving workshops . A side effect has been cheaper household goods and more choice for Kiwis . <p> A survey last year showed New Zealand 's largely subsidy-free @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ countries paid out an estimated $US486 billion ( $NZ772 billion ) in farming subsidies in 2012 . <p> China paid $246 billion in 2012 , mostly to support rice and wheat farmers , Japan $97 billion , the European Union more than $149 billion and the United States $45 billion . Mr McClay says the elimination of export subsidies is significant for New Zealand agriculture exporters , and so it is . <p> As a nation , New Zealand relies heavily on its agricultural sector to drive economic growth . Tourism is a major generator of export earnings , but it is the food New Zealand farmers produce which is world-leading . <p> But while the celebrations are welcome in New Zealand and the Pacific Islands , poorer countries need to be assured their economies are not going to be hurt further by the removal of subsidies in some key areas . <p> Economies like Japan , the United States and China , to a lesser extent , can accommodate the removal of paying people to produce food in an uneconomic way . <p> But those in the Indian sub-continent , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from trade but also climate change and war . <p> The WTO has fallen from grace since former New Zealand prime minister Mike Moore was at its helm . In fact , the organisation was described as broke at the start of the Nairobi conference . <p> Mr McClay says the least developed countries will benefit from new rules improving their ability to engage in international trade . But he acknowledges the package approved is not comprehensive and leaves some important areas to be worked on further . <p> What remains as positive is the seeming willingness of parties to engage on those important issues in the future . 
@@4939818 <h> USA - What 's causing ' freakish ' winter warmth ? <p> The eastern U.S. is warm . Really , really warm . In Central Park , the daytime high on Christmas Eve was 71 degrees Fahrenheit . That 's 8 degrees over the previous record for the day . <p> Similar records are being set all over the east . And it has n't just been a couple days of warm weather ; it 's been weeks . Record high temperatures have been set by the thousands during December so far , and the month is n't over yet . The average December temperature in New York City may reach 14 degrees above average by the end of the month , breaking the previous record , set in 1932 , by a couple of degrees . <p> And it 's not just the heat , it 's the humidity . The warm air is being delivered by southerly winds coming straight from the tropics , and they carry the tropical moisture with them also . The specific humidity -- the total amount of water vapor in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is . The warmth and mugginess are off the charts , separately and together . <p> Because it 's December , this is just a curiosity , a story about weird weather . If these deviations from average were to happen in the summer , though , and they were to last as long as they have over as widespread an area as they have , it would be a heat wave big enough to cause a major national disaster . There would be many deaths , and most likely blackouts and other infrastructure failures . <p> Sooner or later , that will happen . So it 's worth understanding what is going on . What is causing this extreme weather event ? <p> No weather event has a unique cause . Every weather event , this one included , has multiple factors that conspire to make it what it is . <p> The atmosphere is always twisting and turning around , chaotically , with a mind of its own . Any truly extreme event results in large part from that effectively random behavior . For that reason , the specifics @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ time -- weather forecasting , which is the science of predicting this chaotic component , generally does n't work for periods beyond a couple of weeks . <p> But the climate sets the stage , pushing things in one direction or another and influencing the odds that an extreme event of a given type will occur . <p> By making the whole planet warmer , human emissions of greenhouse gases increase the odds of a warm event like this one -- or its more dangerous hypothetical summer counterpart . If we define the event by specifying a fixed threshold -- some number of degrees above average for some number of days , say -- the global warming that we 've already had makes that threshold more likely to be crossed . <p> Or we can look at it another way : If the specific weather situation -- the configuration of the high and low pressure systems , the jet stream , and so forth -- were to have been in the the same state 200 years ago as today , it would have still been warm , but it would n't @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ human influence , the climate also fluctuates naturally . The most important natural fluctuation this winter is El Ni ? o , a change in the state of both the ocean and the atmosphere in the tropical Pacific that comes every few years or so . The coming and going of El Nino events happens on a pace much faster than that of human-induced climate change , but still much slower and steadier than the day-to-day weather , so it makes sense to think of it as another factor gently nudging the weather in one direction . <p> In addition to human-induced climate change , the big El Ni ? o currently in place is very likely a significant factor in the present eastern warmth , because of the way it pushes the jet stream around . That 's why a warm December was predicted well ahead of time ( though not to the extreme that has happened ) ; we knew through the fall that the El Ni ? o would still be in place now , and what its effects on the U.S. tend to be . <p> El @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ any more than global warming does . There 's still a lot of wiggle room for the atmosphere to do its own thing . That 's very apparent in the Pacific Northwest , where I am now . Normally an El Ni ? o makes it dry here , but instead it 's been the rainiest December in history . So the atmosphere 's own variability must be important as well . Each of these factors is present , and likely playing some role in the specific event we see transpiring now . <p> But just saying that every possible cause matters to an extreme weather event is n't very satisfying . Which cause matters the most , by how much and in what sense ? Can we measure these various influences on the weather with numbers ? <p> In principle , we can . There is now a growing scientific research area that attempts to do this called " extreme event attribution . " Attribution studies try to quantify the influence of specific causes ( including , but not limited to human-induced climate change ) on some individual weather events @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has historically been about . The National Academy of Sciences is currently performing a study on this relatively new and rapidly evolving field . ( Full disclosure : I 'm on the committee that is performing the study . ) <p> This warm event is still ongoing , so attribution studies have n't been done on it yet . But they will come . The science is reaching the point where we can make real scientific statements about the factors influencing individual weather events like this one , with numbers and statistical confidence intervals and multiple lines of evidence . <p> So is the current eastern warmth due to human-induced climate change ? How about the massive El Ni ? o event that is currently underway in the tropical Pacific ? Or is it just an extreme random fluctuation of the weather ? <p> Detailed answers have to await the research to be done . But the basic , general answer , glib but likely true , is : Yes . <h> Comments <p> They must have had that dreaded climate change thingy at the same time of the year in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Ashland Kansas , Geary Oklahoma and Encinal Texas were all over 90 degrees F. @@4941690 <h> NZ agriculture well placed for 2016 <p> It is hard to beat New Zealand 's efficiencies in producing grass fed meat and milk . <p> OPINION : Three pieces of news that appeared at the end of 2015 should not be forgotten in 2016 . <p> Firstly , the climate change talks in Paris included efficiencies of food production : There is no substitute for food , so which countries can produce most food with least greenhouse gas impact ? <p> For grass fed meat and milk , New Zealand is hard to beat . The increasing integration between the dairy and beef sectors is part of the advantage , as is the longevity of dairy cows in the herd , and the judicious use of supplementary feeding . <p> Secondly , the World Trade Organisation has achieved progress on removing agricultural subsidies . Admittedly the removal is in the smallest component , ' export ' subsidies , but the progress is a sign for the future . <p> Export subsidies are put in place to assist domestic producers in exporting to ' other countries ' . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a glut on the domestic market . They cost the rest of society through increased taxation , but create employment . For the domestic consumer , the export subsidies mean that domestic prices for food are higher than they might be in a free market . <p> Of greater importance is that any form of subsidy assists food price competition where some players can compete only because of the subsidies -- subsidies support their inefficiencies . New Zealand 's farmers and producers , who are efficient and who are not subsidised , are not competing on a level playing field . The acknowledgement that subsidies skew the competition , coming in the same year as significant progress on the Trans Pacific Partnership Agreement , is good news for primary production exports . The world is recognising that food is a global resource and the impacts of its production are shared . Allowing market forces to operate , rather than trade barriers and subsidies , puts New Zealand in pole position . <p> The New Zealand public might still require convincing about the importance of agriculture , but the third piece of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Zealand ranked ninth of 180 countries in the United Nations ' Human Development Index . Life expectancy , access to education and income per capita are combined in the index , and New Zealand has steadily climbed the ranks since 1990 . <p> Of the components , per capita income is the least high ranking score -- and without primary production our ranking would be distinctly lower : farming , fisheries , forestry , horticulture and viticulture directly provide over 8 per cent gross domestic product and 42 per cent of exports ; indirectly the contribution is much higher . <p> The human development index supports other reports : New Zealand was fifth place in the social progress index at the beginning of the year , ninth in the United Nations ' world happiness index , and eighth in the OECD 's better life lndex , reflecting high satisfaction with community , air and water quality , health , safety and education . <p> In November the Legatum prosperity index ranked New Zealand fourth of 142 countries , and top of the ' social capital ' sub-index factoring in tolerance , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ place to live and the indications are that it will get even better -- roll on 2016. 
@@4941725 <h> Expat Tales : Happy hours in Hamburg <p> Feilidh O'Dwyer-Strang moved to Hamburg to complete his Master 's in Journalism and Globalisation . <p> Feilidh O'Dwyer-Strang says Hamburg is a compact and affordable option compared to other European cities . <p> What inspired your move , and how long have you been there ? I came to Hamburg to complete my thesis for an Erasmus Mundus Master 's in Journalism and Globalisation . I 've been here for three months . <p> What do you do there ? I 'm a student at the University of Hamburg but I also write for and edit a climate change blog for the university and occasionally represent Otago University at international student recruitment events . <h> Kiwi expats : What 's your tale ? <p> Share your stories , photos and videos . <p> What are the greatest advantages to living there ? It 's a spectacular city . It has amazing architecture , the Alster , cool areas to party and the public transport is excellent . It 's relatively compact compared to Berlin and has plenty of nice @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ other European cities . <p> Disadvantages ? The weather is n't too flash but one could say that about most of Northern Europe . My German is still in the early stages and although most people here speak very good English , there have been times when I was navigating Germany 's bureaucratic madness and I wished I was a bit more fluent with Deutsch . <p> How expensive is it compared to New Zealand ? How much is a beer ? Food and going out is far cheaper than NZ . You can buy a litre of milk for about 55 Euro cents ( less than $1 ) and a pint at a bar wo n't be more than ? 3.50 . Rents are similar to Auckland but it depends on what suburb you live in . <p> What do you do in your spare time ? Practise my German , socialise , read and watch a lot of politics and news , exercise and travel as much as I can . <p> What 's the local delicacy and would you recommend eating it ? The delicacy I recommend is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ they call a mexikaner - which was apparently invented here . It 's kind of like a bloody mary : tomato juice , tabasco , pepper and salt mixed with vodka in a shot glass . I find it quite delicious but I notice a lot of people wrinkling their noses when they down one . <p> Easiest way to get around ? Public transport or bike . The city is quite cycle-friendly with designated biking lanes on most streets . Public transport is great - you can go basically anywhere at any time . <p> What 's the shopping like ? I 've hit a couple of nice clothing shops around town but I tend to shop only when I need something rather than just perusing . <p> Best after-dark activity ? After-dark you should take a stroll along the Reeperbahn . To my knowledge , it 's one of the biggest party streets in Europe . You have a kilometre or so of bars , clubs , outlandish sex shops and general debauchery . It 's a very wide street and jam-packed on a Friday or Saturday night . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ seeing - just to feel the atmosphere . <p> Best time of year to visit ? I have n't been here in summer yet , but the locals tell me it 's beautiful . I believe it starts getting hotter around May/June . <p> What are the top three things you recommend for visitors ? 1 . Go to Miniatur Wunderland - it 's a spectacular miniature museum that recreates various cities from around the world with intricately crafted figurines , moving vehicles , scale-model buildings etc . The designers have a sense of humour , so there are lot of little surprises if you keep your eyes peeled . <p> 2 . Spend at least three nights in Hamburg . There really is a lot to see . You 've got a great mix of culture and partying . <p> 3 . I have n't done it yet but I 've heard that going along to a game of F.C St. Pauli is a pretty chill experience . They are currently close to the top of the second division in the German Bundesliga . They are a very progressive club @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ family and friends , what do you miss most about home ? It 's pretty hard to beat the great outdoors back home . There is nice nature here but I miss some of NZ 's beaches and forests . I also miss my motorbike , which I sold to come here , and being able to buy mince pies . <p> How easy is it for you to get back to NZ ? Not so easy . The cost and time it takes ( approx 30 hours door-to-door ) to get back home are a little prohibitive . In the last year and a half , I 've been back home once . That was lovely but I had already started to feel like a tourist at home . <p> For Kiwis looking to move there , which industries are seeking fresh talent ?
